# Liverpool

This article is about the city in England. For the namesake football club, see [Liverpool F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_F.C.) For other uses, see [Liverpool (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_\(disambiguation\)).

**Liverpool** is a [port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port) [city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_status_in_the_United_Kingdom) and [metropolitan borough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_borough) in [Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside), England. It is situated on the eastern side of the [Mersey Estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Mersey), near the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea), 178 miles (286 km) north-west of [London](17867.md). It had an estimated population of 507,915 in 2025.[^cite_note-popstats-4] The city is within the larger [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region), a [combined authority area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_authority) with a population of over 1.5 million.[^cite_note-ONS_GVA_and_GDP-6]

Established as a borough in [Lancashire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancashire) in 1207, Liverpool became significant in the late 17th century when the [Port of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Liverpool) was heavily involved in the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md). The port also imported cotton for the [Lancashire textile mills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_manufacture_during_the_British_Industrial_Revolution), and became a major departure point for English and Irish emigrants to North America. Liverpool rose to global economic importance at the forefront of the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) in the 19th century and was home to the [first inter-city railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_and_Manchester_Railway), the first non-combustible warehouse system (the [Royal Albert Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Dock,_Liverpool)), and a pioneering [elevated electrical railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Overhead_Railway); it was granted city status in 1880 and was moved from Lancashire to the newly created county of Merseyside in [1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Government_Act_1972). It entered a period of decline in the mid-20th century, which was largely reversed after the [European Union](9317.md) selected it as the [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture) for 2008,[^cite_note-7] reportedly generating over £800 million for the local economy within a year.[^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9]

The [economy of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Liverpool) is diverse and encompasses tourism, [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Liverpool), [maritime](28769.md), [hospitality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospitality_industry), [healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_industry), [life sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_life_sciences), advanced manufacturing, [creative](674483.md) and [digital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_economy) sectors.[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12] The city is home to the UK's second highest number of [art galleries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_galleries), [national museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museums_Liverpool), [listed buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Listed_buildings_in_Liverpool), and [parks and open spaces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parks_and_open_spaces_in_Liverpool), behind only London.[^cite_note-visitliverpool-13] It is often used as a filming location due to its [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Liverpool) and was the fifth most visited UK city by foreign tourists in 2022.[^cite_note-14] It has produced numerous [musicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Liverpool), most notably [the Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles), and recording artists from the city have had more UK No. 1 singles than anywhere else in the world.[^cite_note-15] It has also produced numerous [academics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Academics_from_Liverpool), [actors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Actors_from_Liverpool), [artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Artists_from_Liverpool), [comedians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Comedians_from_Liverpool), [filmmakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Film_people_from_Liverpool), [poets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Poets_from_Liverpool), [scientists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Scientists_from_Liverpool), [sportspeople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Sportspeople_from_Liverpool), and [writers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Writers_from_Liverpool). It is the home of [Premier League](11250.md) football teams [Everton](91155.md) and [Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_F.C.). The world's oldest still-operating main line railway station, [Liverpool Lime Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Lime_Street_railway_station), is in the city centre; it is also served by the underground [Merseyrail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyrail) network. The city's port was the fourth largest in the UK in 2023, with numerous shipping and freight lines having headquarters and offices there.[^cite_note-16]

Residents of Liverpool are formally known as Liverpudlians but are more often called Scousers in reference to [scouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobscouse), a local stew made popular by sailors. The city's [distinct local accent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scouse) is also primarily known as Scouse. Its cultural and ethnic diversity is the result of attracting immigrants from various areas, particularly Ireland, Scandinavia, and Wales; it is also home to [the UK's oldest black community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people_in_Liverpool) and [Europe's oldest Chinese community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Liverpool), as well as the first [mosque](19894.md) in England.[^cite_note-17]

## Toponymy

The name comes from the [Old English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_English) *lifer*, meaning thick or muddy water, and *pōl*, meaning a pool or creek, and is first recorded around 1190 as *Liuerpul*.[^cite_note-18][^cite_note-DH-19] According to the *Cambridge Dictionary of English Place-Names*, "The original reference was to a pool or tidal creek now filled up into which two streams drained".[^cite_note-20] Other origins of the name have been suggested, including "elverpool", a reference to the large number of eels in the [Mersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Mersey).[^cite_note-scouse-21] The adjective "Liverpudlian" was first recorded in 1833.[^cite_note-DH-19]

## History

Main articles: [History of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Liverpool) and [Timeline of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Liverpool)



The earliest-known image of Liverpool, in 1680



A map of Liverpool's original seven streets (north to the left)



[Bluecoat Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluecoat_Chambers), completed in 1725, the oldest surviving building in [Liverpool city centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_city_centre)

### Early history

In the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), Liverpool first existed as farmland within the [West Derby Hundred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Derby_Hundred)[^cite_note-22] before growing into a small town of farmers, fishermen and tradesmen and tactical army base for [King John of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_of_England). A tidal creek, known as [the Pool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pool,_Liverpool) provided a natural harbour for ships.[^cite_note-23] The town was planned with its own [castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Castle), although due to outbreaks of disease and its subordinance to the nearby [Roman port of Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deva_Victrix), the town's growth and prosperity stagnated until the late 17th and early 18th centuries. There was substantial growth in the mid- to late 18th century, when the town became the European port most heavily involved in the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md).[^cite_note-24]

[King John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_of_England)'s [letters patent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letters_patent) of 1207 announced the foundation of the borough of Liverpool (then spelt as *Liuerpul*). There is no evidence that the place had previously been a centre of any trade. The borough was probably created because King John decided that it would be a convenient place to embark men and supplies for his [Irish campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John's_first_expedition_to_Ireland): in particular his [Irish campaign of 1209](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lordship_of_Ireland).[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-BHOLderhun-26] The original street plan of Liverpool is said to have been designed by King John near the same time it was granted a [royal charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_charter), making it a borough. The original seven streets were laid out in the shape of a double cross: Bank Street (now [Water Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_Street,_Liverpool)), [Castle Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Street,_Liverpool), [Chapel Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapel_Street,_Liverpool), [Dale Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Street), Juggler Street (now [High Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Street,_Liverpool)), Moor Street (now [Tithebarn Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tithebarn_Street)) and Whiteacre Street (now [Old Hall Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Hall_Street)).[^cite_note-BHOLderhun-26] [Liverpool Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Castle) was built before 1235, and survived until it was demolished in the 1720s.[^cite_note-BHOLcastle-27] By the middle of the 16th century, the population was still around 600, although this was likely to have fallen from an earlier peak of 1,000 people due to slow trade and the effects of the [plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1557_influenza_pandemic).[^cite_note-28][^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30]

In the 17th century, there was slow progress in trade and population growth. Battles for control of the town were waged during the [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War), including a brief siege in 1644.[^cite_note-31] In 1699, the same year as its first recorded [slave ship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_ship), *Liverpool Merchant*, set sail for Africa,[^cite_note-32] Liverpool was made a parish by [Act of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Parliament). But arguably, the legislation of 1695 that reformed the Liverpool council was of more significance to its subsequent development.[^cite_note-33] Since [Roman times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Britain) nearby [Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester) on the [River Dee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Dee,_Wales) had been the region's principal port on the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea). However, as the Dee began to [silt up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silt), maritime trade from Chester became increasingly difficult and shifted towards Liverpool on the neighbouring [River Mersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Mersey). The first of the [Liverpool docks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Liverpool) was constructed in 1715, and the system of docks gradually grew into a large interconnected system.[^cite_note-34]

As trade from the [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies), including sugar, surpassed that of Ireland and Europe, and as the [River Dee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Dee,_Wales) continued to silt up, Liverpool began to grow even faster. The first commercial [wet dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_dock) was built in Liverpool in 1715.[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] Substantial profits from the [slave trade](49005.md) and tobacco helped the town to prosper and rapidly grow, although several prominent local men, including [William Rathbone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rathbone_IV), [William Roscoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Roscoe) and [Edward Rushton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Rushton), were at the forefront of the [local abolitionist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-37]

### 19th century



Inaugural journey of the [Liverpool and Manchester Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_and_Manchester_Railway) in 1830



[Lime Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lime_Street,_Liverpool), Liverpool, in the 1890s, [St.George's Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._George's_Hall,_Liverpool) to the left, [Great North Western Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_North_Western_Hotel) to the right, [Walker Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walker_Art_Gallery) and [Sessions House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sessions_House,_Liverpool) in the background. Statues of [Prince Albert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert,_Prince_Consort), [Disraeli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli), [Queen Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Victoria) and [Wellington's Column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington's_Column) in the middle ground.

The 19th century saw Liverpool rise to global economic importance. Pioneering arose, and [world-first](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Liverpool) technology and civic facilities launched in the city to serve the accelerating population, one that was fuelled by an influx of ethnic and religious communities from all around the world.

&#x20;By the start of the 19th century, a large volume of trade was passing through Liverpool, and the construction of major buildings reflected this wealth. In 1830, Liverpool and [Manchester](20206.md) became the first cities to have an intercity rail link, through the [Liverpool and Manchester Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_and_Manchester_Railway). The population continued to rise rapidly, especially during the 1840s when [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_diaspora) migrants began arriving by the hundreds of thousands as a result of the [Great Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Potato_Famine_\(1845%E2%80%931849\)). While many Irish people settled in the city at that time, a large percentage also emigrated to the United States or moved to the industrial centres of [Lancashire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancashire), Yorkshire, and the [Midlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midlands).[^cite_note-38]

Wikisource logo

English [Wikisource](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikisource) has original text related to this article:&#x20;

**['Liverpool', a poetical\
illustration by L. E. L.](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/en\:Letitia%20Elizabeth%20Landon%20\(L.%20E.%20L.\)%20in%20Fisher's%20Drawing%20Room%20Scrap%20Book,%201833/Liverpool)**

In her poetical illustration "Liverpool" (1832), which celebrates the city's worldwide commerce, [Letitia Elizabeth Landon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letitia_Elizabeth_Landon) refers specifically to the [Macgregor Laird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macgregor_Laird) expedition to the Niger River, at that time in progress.[^cite_note-39] This is accompanied by a painting by [Samuel Austin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Austin_\(artist\)), *Liverpool, from the Mersey*.[^cite_note-40]

Britain was a major market for cotton imported from the [Deep South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_South) of the United States, which fed the [textile industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_manufacture_during_the_British_Industrial_Revolution) in the country. Given the crucial place cotton held in the city's economy, during the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War) Liverpool was, in the words of historian [Sven Beckert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sven_Beckert), "the most pro-Confederate place in the world outside the [Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America) itself".[^cite_note-41] Liverpool merchants helped to bring out cotton from ports blockaded by the [Union Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Navy), built ships of war for the [Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America), and supplied the [South](179553.md) with military equipment and credit.[^cite_note-42]

During the war, the [Confederate Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_States_Navy) ship, the [CSS *Alabama*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSS_Alabama), was built at [Birkenhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkenhead) on the Mersey, and the [CSS *Shenandoah*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSS_Shenandoah) surrendered there (being the final surrender at the end of the war). The city was also the centre of Confederate purchases of war materiel, including arms and ammunition, uniforms, and naval supplies to be smuggled by [British blockade runners to the South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockade_runners_of_the_American_Civil_War).[^cite_note-43]

For periods during the 19th century, the wealth of Liverpool exceeded that of London,[^cite_note-Ten_facts_about_Liverpool-44] and Liverpool's [Custom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Custom_House,_Liverpool) was the single largest contributor to the [British Exchequer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Exchequer).[^cite_note-45] Liverpool was the only British city ever to have its own [Whitehall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitehall) office.[^cite_note-46] During this century, at least 40% of the world's entire trade passed through Liverpool.[^cite_note-47]

In the early 19th century, Liverpool played a major role in the [Antarctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antarctic) [sealing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_hunting) industry, in recognition of which [Liverpool Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Beach) in the [South Shetland Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Shetland_Islands) is named after the city.[^cite_note-48]

As early as 1851, the city was described as "the New York of Europe".[^cite_note-49] During the late 19th and early 20th centuries, Liverpool was attracting immigrants from across Europe. This resulted in the construction of a diverse array of religious buildings in the city for the new ethnic and religious groups, many of which are still in use today. The [Deutsche Kirche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Church,_Liverpool), [Greek Orthodox Church of St Nicholas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_St_Nicholas), [Gustav Adolf Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Adolf_Church,_Liverpool) and [Princes Road Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princes_Road_Synagogue) were all established in the 1800s to serve Liverpool's growing German, Greek, Nordic and Jewish communities, respectively. One of Liverpool's oldest surviving churches, [St. Peter's Roman Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Peter's_Roman_Catholic_Church,_Liverpool), served the [Polish community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poles_in_the_United_Kingdom) in its final years as a place of worship.

### 20th century



Liverpool's Lime Street area pictured from above in 1946

The 20th century saw Liverpool's established rank as a global economic powerhouse challenged. Its strategic location as an international seaport made it particularly vulnerable in two [World wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_war). [Economic depressions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_depression) (both in the United Kingdom and across the world), changing [housing patterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_in_the_United_Kingdom) and [containerisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containerisation) in the maritime industry contributed to a downtrend in the city's productivity and prosperity. Despite this, the city's influence on global popular culture excelled and by the end of the century, the continuing process of [urban renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_renewal) paved the way for the redefined modern city of the 21st century.

The period after the [Great War](4764461.md) was marked by social unrest, as society grappled with the massive war losses of young men, as well as trying to re-integrate veterans into civilian life and the economy. Unemployment and poor living standards greeted many ex-servicemen. [Union organising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_unions_in_the_United_Kingdom) and [strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1926_United_Kingdom_general_strike) took place in numerous locations, including a [police strike in Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_police_strikes_in_1918_and_1919) among the [City Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside_Police). Numerous [colonial soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) and sailors from Africa and India, who had served with the [British Armed Forces](31730.md), settled in Liverpool and other port cities. In June 1919, they were subject to attack by whites in racial riots; residents in the port included [Swedish immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedes_in_the_United_Kingdom), and both groups had to compete with native people from Liverpool for jobs and housing. In this period, race riots also took place in other port cities.[^cite_note-Tabili-50]

The [Housing, Town Planning, \&c. Act 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing,_Town_Planning,_\&c._Act_1919) resulted in mass council housing being built across Liverpool during the 1920s and 1930s. In the 1920s and 1930s, as much as 15% of the city's population (around 140,000 people) was relocated from the inner-city to new purpose built, lower density suburban housing estates, based on the belief that this would improve their standard of living, though the overall benefits have been contested.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] Numerous private homes were also built during this era. During the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) of the early 1930s, unemployment peaked at around 30% in the city. Liverpool was the site of Britain's first [provincial airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_John_Lennon_Airport), operating from 1930.

During the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War), the critical strategic importance of Liverpool was recognised by both [Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler) and [Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill). The city was heavily bombed by the Germans, suffering a [blitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Blitz) second only to London's.[^cite_note-53] The pivotal [Battle of the Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Atlantic) was planned, fought and won from Liverpool.[^cite_note-54]

The *[Luftwaffe](17885.md)* made 80 [air raids on Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Blitz), killing 2,500 people and causing damage to almost half the homes in the metropolitan area. Significant rebuilding followed the war, including massive housing estates and the [Seaforth Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seaforth_Dock), the largest dock project in Britain. Since 1952, Liverpool has been twinned with [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne), Germany, a city that also suffered severe aerial bombing during the war. In the 1950s and 1960s, much of the immediate reconstruction that took place in the city centre proved to be deeply unpopular. The historic portions of the city that had survived German bombing suffered extensive destruction during urban renewal. It has been argued that the so-called "Shankland Plan" of the 1960s, named after the town planner [Graeme Shankland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graeme_Shankland), led to compromised town planning and vast road-building schemes that devastated and divided inner city neighbourhoods. Concrete [brutalist architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brutalist_architecture), compromised visions, botched projects and grand designs that were never realised became the subject of condemnation. Historian [Raphael Samuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael_Samuel) labelled Graeme Shankland "the butcher of Liverpool".[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58]

A [West Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies) black community has existed in the city since the first two decades of the 20th century. Like most British cities and industrialised towns, Liverpool became home to [Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) immigrants, beginning after World War I with colonial soldiers and sailors who had served in the area. More immigrants arrived after World War II, mostly settling in older inner-city areas such as [Toxteth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toxteth), where housing was less expensive. The black population of Liverpool was recorded at 1.90% in 2011. In the [2021 Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_census), 5.2% described themselves as black African, Caribbean, mixed white and black African, mixed white and Caribbean or 'other black'.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60]



[Mathew Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathew_Street) is one of many tourist attractions related to the Beatles, and the location of [The Cavern Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Cavern_Club) and [Liverpool Wall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Wall_of_Fame).

In the 1960s, Liverpool was the centre of the "[Merseybeat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beat_music)" sound whose best-known band is [the Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles). [Liverpool Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_John_Lennon_Airport) was renamed after Beatle and Liverpudlian [John Lennon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lennon) in 2002, the first British airport to be named in honour of an individual.[^cite_note-fola-61][^cite_note-62]

Previously part of Lancashire, and a county borough from 1889, Liverpool became a [metropolitan borough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_borough) within the newly created metropolitan county of [Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside), in 1974. From the mid-1970s onwards, Liverpool's docks and traditional [manufacturing industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manufacturing_industries) declined due to restructuring of shipping and heavy industry, causing massive losses of jobs. The advent of [containerisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containerisation) meant that the city's docks became largely obsolete, and dock workers were made unemployed. By the early 1980s, unemployment rates in Liverpool were among the highest in the UK,[^cite_note-63] standing at 17% by January 1982 although, this was about half the level of unemployment that had affected the city during the Great Depression some 50 years previously.[^cite_note-64] During this period, Liverpool became a hub of fierce left-wing opposition to the central government in London.[^cite_note-65] Liverpool in the 1980s has been labelled as Britain's 'shock city'. Once the acclaimed second city of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) which rivalled the capital city in global significance, Liverpool had collapsed in to its 'nadir' at the depths of [post-colonial](302084.md), [post-industrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-industrial_society) Britain.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] In the late 20th century, Liverpool's economy began to recover. The late 1980s saw the opening of a regenerated [Albert Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Dock,_Liverpool) which proved to be a catalyst for further regeneration.[^cite_note-68] In the mid-1990s, the city enjoyed growth rates higher than the national average. At the end of the 20th century, Liverpool was concentrating on regeneration, a process that continues today.

### 21st century



The [Liverpool Cruise Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cruise_Terminal) and surrounding office and residential developments, part of the [Liverpool Waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Waters) megaproject

|  | This section **may lend [undue weight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Neutral_point_of_view#Due_and_undue_weight) to certain ideas, incidents, or controversies**. Relevant discussion may be found on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Liverpool#). Please help [improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Liverpool\&action=edit) by rewriting it in a [balanced fashion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Neutral_point_of_view#Balance) that contextualises different points of view. *(September 2026)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Ongoing regeneration combined with the hosting of internationally significant events has helped to re-purpose Liverpool as one of the most visited, tourist orientated, cities in the United Kingdom. City leaders are focussing on long-term strategies to grow the city's population and economy, while national government explores the continuous potential for devolution in the city.

In 2002, Queen [Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_II) and [Prince Philip, Duke of Edinburgh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Philip,_Duke_of_Edinburgh) visited Liverpool to mark the [Golden Jubilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Jubilee_of_Elizabeth_II). On speaking to an audience at [Liverpool Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Town_Hall), the Queen recognised Liverpool as "one of the most distinctive and energetic parts of the United Kingdom", and paid tribute to the city's "major orchestras, world-class museums and galleries". She also acknowledged Liverpool's bid to become the [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture).[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] To celebrate the [Golden Jubilee of Elizabeth II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Jubilee_of_Elizabeth_II) in 2002, the conservation charity [Plantlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantlife) organised a competition to choose [county flowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_flower); the [sea-holly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eryngium_maritimum) was Liverpool's final choice. The initiative was designed to highlight growing threats to the UK's flower species and also ask the public about which flowers best represented their county.[^cite_note-71]

Capitalising on the popularity of 1960s rock groups, such as [the Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles), as well as the city's world-class art galleries, museums and landmarks, tourism and culture have become a significant factor in Liverpool's economy.



Modern developments on the Liverpool Waterfront

In 2004, property developer [Grosvenor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grosvenor_Group) started the [Paradise Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_One), a £920 million development based on [Paradise Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradise_Street,_Liverpool). This produced one of the most significant changes to Liverpool's city centre since the post-war reconstruction. Renamed as '[Liverpool One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_One),' the centre opened in May 2008.

In 2007, events and celebrations took place in honour of the 800th anniversary of the founding of the borough of Liverpool. Liverpool was designated as a joint [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture) for 2008. The celebrations included the erection of [La Princesse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Princesse), a large mechanical spider 20 metres high and weighing 37 tonnes, which represented the "eight legs" of Liverpool: honour, history, music, the Mersey, the ports, governance, sunshine and culture. La Princesse roamed the streets of the city during the festivities, and concluded by entering the [Queensway Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queensway_Tunnel).

Spearheaded by the multi-billion-[pound](270673.md) Liverpool ONE development, regeneration continued throughout the 2010s. Some of the most significant redevelopment projects included new buildings in the [Commercial District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Centre#Commercial_District), [King's Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King's_Dock,_Port_of_Liverpool), [Mann Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mann_Island), around [Lime Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lime_Street,_Liverpool), the [Baltic Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Triangle), [RopeWalks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RopeWalks,_Liverpool), and [Edge Lane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edge_Lane).[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74]



Headquarters of [Liverpool City Region Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region_Combined_Authority), which invests in Liverpool's major infrastructure and regeneration projects

Changes to Liverpool's governance took place in 2014. The local authority of [Liverpool City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council) decided to pool its power and resources with surrounding boroughs through the formation of the [Liverpool City Region Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region_Combined_Authority) in a form of [devolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom). With a devolved budget granted by [central government](25318118.md), the authority now oversees and invests in foremost strategic affairs throughout the [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region), including major regeneration projects. The authority, along with Liverpool City Council itself, has embarked on long-term plans to grow the population and economy of the city.[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78]

By the 2020s, urban regeneration throughout the city continues. [Liverpool Waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Waters), a mixed-use development in the city's disused northern docklands, has been identified as one of the largest megaprojects in the UK's history. [Everton's new stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everton_Stadium) at [Bramley-Moore Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bramley-Moore_Dock) was regarded as the largest single-site private sector development in the United Kingdom at the time of construction.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80]

Major events, business and political conferences regularly take place in the city and form an important part of the economy. In June 2014, Prime Minister [David Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cameron) launched the International Festival for Business in Liverpool, the world's largest business event in 2014,[^cite_note-81] and the largest in the UK since the [Festival of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festival_of_Britain) in 1951.[^cite_note-82] The [Labour Party](19279158.md) has chosen Liverpool numerous times since the mid-2010s for their annual [Labour Party Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_Party_Conference). Liverpool hosted the [Eurovision Song Contest 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2023).

In the early to mid-2020s, Liverpool saw several notable demonstrations on social and political issues. Between September and November 2022, about 600 workers at the [Port of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Liverpool) held a series of strikes over pay.[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84] In October 2023, following the [7 October attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7_October_attacks), protests linked to the [Israel–Gaza conflict](16346266.md) took place in Liverpool, including a city-centre march,[^cite_note-85] a [student occupation and encampment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_war_protests_at_universities) by Palestine protesters at the [University of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Liverpool),[^cite_note-86] and further protests and marches that continued into 2024.[^cite_note-87] In August 2024, anti-immigration and anti-racism demonstrations were accompanied by violent disorder.[^cite_note-88] In 2025 and 2026, protests by [Palestine](241405.md) supporters over the UK's ban on [Palestine Action](67777543.md), a group proscribed under the [Terrorism Act 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism_Act_2000), led to 66 arrests and more than 50 arrests respectively in Liverpool.[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90]

### Inventions and innovations



The [Liverpool School of Tropical Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_School_of_Tropical_Medicine), the first such school in the world

Liverpool has been a centre of invention and innovation. Railways, transatlantic [steamships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamship), municipal trams,[^cite_note-91] and electric trains were all pioneered in Liverpool as modes of mass transit. In 1829 and 1836, the first railway tunnels in the world were constructed under Liverpool ([Wapping Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wapping_Tunnel)). From 1950 to 1951, the world's first scheduled passenger helicopter service ran between Liverpool and [Cardiff](5882.md).[^cite_note-92]

The first [School for the Blind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_School_for_the_Blind_\(Liverpool\)),[^cite_note-93] [Mechanics' Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanics'_Institutes),[^cite_note-94] High School for Girls,[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96] council house,[^cite_note-97] and Juvenile Court[^cite_note-98] were all founded in Liverpool. Charities such as the [RSPCA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSPCA),[^cite_note-99] [NSPCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSPCC),[^cite_note-100] [Age Concern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_Concern),[^cite_note-101] [Relate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relate), and [Citizen's Advice Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizen's_Advice_Bureau)[^cite_note-102] all evolved from work in the city.

The first [lifeboat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lifeboat_\(rescue\)) station, public bath and wash-house,[^cite_note-103] sanitary act,[^cite_note-104] medical officer for health ([William Henry Duncan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Henry_Duncan)), district nurse, [slum clearance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slum_clearance_in_the_United_Kingdom),[^cite_note-105] purpose-built ambulance,[^cite_note-106] X-ray medical diagnosis,[^cite_note-107] school of tropical medicine ([Liverpool School of Tropical Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_School_of_Tropical_Medicine)), motorised municipal fire-engine,[^cite_note-108] free school meal,[^cite_note-109] cancer research centre,[^cite_note-110] and [zoonosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoonosis) research centre[^cite_note-111] all originated in Liverpool. The first British Nobel Prize was awarded in 1902 to [Ronald Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Ross), professor at the School of Tropical Medicine, the first school of its kind in the world.[^cite_note-112] [Orthopaedic surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthopaedic_surgery) was pioneered in Liverpool by [Hugh Owen Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Owen_Thomas),[^cite_note-113] and modern medical anaesthetics by [Thomas Cecil Gray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Cecil_Gray).

The world's first integrated [sewer system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_water_supply_and_sanitation) was constructed in Liverpool by [James Newlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Newlands), appointed in 1847 as the UK's first borough engineer.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115] Liverpool also founded the UK's first [Underwriters'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Underwriting) Association[^cite_note-116] and the first [Institute of Accountants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_Chartered_Accountants_in_England_and_Wales). The Western world's first financial derivatives (cotton futures) were traded on the [Liverpool Cotton Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Cotton_Association) in the late 1700s.[^cite_note-117]



[Oriel Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriel_Chambers), the first "modern" building in the world with its iron-framed [curtain-wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtain_wall_\(architecture\))

In the arts, Liverpool was home to the first lending library ([The Lyceum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lyceum,_Liverpool)), athenaeum society ([Liverpool Athenaeum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Athenaeum)), arts centre ([Bluecoat Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluecoat_Chambers)),[^cite_note-118] and public art conservation centre ([National Conservation Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Conservation_Centre)).[^cite_note-119] It is also home to the UK's oldest surviving classical orchestra ([Royal Liverpool Philharmonic Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Liverpool_Philharmonic_Orchestra))[^cite_note-120] and repertory theatre ([Liverpool Playhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Playhouse)).[^cite_note-121]

In 1864, [Peter Ellis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Ellis_\(architect\)) built the world's first iron-framed, [curtain-walled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtain_wall_\(architecture\)) office building, [Oriel Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriel_Chambers), which has become a defining feature of skyscrapers around the world.[^cite_note-122] The UK's first purpose-built department store was [Compton House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compton_House,_Liverpool), completed in 1867 for the retailer J.R. Jeffrey.[^cite_note-123] It was the largest store in the world at the time.[^cite_note-124]



[Lewis's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis's) department store on Ranelagh Street. In 1879, the [Christmas grotto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa's_workshop) was conceived in the store.

Between 1862 and 1867, Liverpool held an annual [Grand Olympic Festival](22576.md#Forerunners). Devised by [John Hulley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hulley) and [Charles Pierre Melly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Pierre_Melly), these games were the first to be wholly amateur in nature and international in outlook.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] The programme of the first modern Olympiad in [Athens](1216.md) in 1896 was almost identical to that of the Liverpool Olympics.[^cite_note-127] In 1865, Hulley co-founded the National Olympian Association in Liverpool, a forerunner of the [British Olympic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Olympic_Association). Its articles of foundation provided the framework for the [Olympic Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Charter).

A concept devised by retail entrepreneur [David Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Lewis_\(English_merchant\)), the first [Christmas grotto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa's_workshop) opened in [Lewis's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis's) department store in Liverpool in 1879.[^cite_note-128] Sir [Alfred Lewis Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Lewis_Jones), a shipowner, introduced bananas to the UK via Liverpool's docks in 1884.[^cite_note-129] The [Mersey Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mersey_Railway), opened in 1886, incorporated the world's first tunnel under a tidal [estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estuary)[^cite_note-130] and the world's first deep-level underground stations ([Liverpool James Street railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_James_Street_railway_station)).



Liverpool was the first city outside London to be chosen to have an official [Blue plaque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_plaque) and now has the largest number outside London.

[^cite_note-131]

In 1889, borough engineer [John Alexander Brodie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Alexander_Brodie) invented the football goal net. He was also a pioneer in the use of [pre-fabricated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-fabricated) housing[^cite_note-132] and oversaw the construction of the UK's first ring road ([A5058](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A5058_road)) and intercity highway ([East Lancashire Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Lancashire_Road)), as well as the [Queensway Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queensway_Tunnel) linking Liverpool and [Birkenhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkenhead). Described as "the eighth wonder of the world" at the time of its construction, it was the longest underwater tunnel in the world for 24 years.

In 1897, the [Lumière brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_and_Louis_Lumi%C3%A8re) filmed Liverpool,[^cite_note-133] including what is believed to be the world's first [tracking shot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tracking_shot),[^cite_note-134] taken from the [Liverpool Overhead Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Overhead_Railway), the world's first elevated electrified railway. The Overhead Railway was the first railway in the world to use [electric multiple units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_multiple_unit), employ automatic signalling, and install an escalator.

Liverpool inventor [Frank Hornby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Hornby) was a visionary in toy development and manufacture, producing three of the most popular lines of toys in the 20th century: [Meccano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meccano), [Hornby Model Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hornby_Model_Railways) (both in 1901), and [Dinky Toys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dinky_Toys) in 1934.[^cite_note-135] The [British Interplanetary Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Interplanetary_Society), founded in Liverpool in 1933 by Phillip Ellaby Cleator, is the world's oldest existing organisation devoted to the promotion of [spaceflight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spaceflight). Its journal, the *[Journal of the British Interplanetary Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_the_British_Interplanetary_Society)*, is the longest-running astronautical publication in the world.[^cite_note-136]

In 1999, Liverpool was the first city outside London to be awarded [blue plaques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_plaques) by [English Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Heritage) in recognition of the "significant contribution made by its sons and daughters in all walks of life".[^cite_note-137]

## Government

See also: [Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside) and [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region)



The [Cunard Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cunard_Building) (left), housing the main offices of [Liverpool City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council)

For the purposes of [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_England), Liverpool is classified as a [metropolitan borough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_borough) with city status. The metropolitan borough is located within both the county of [Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside) and the [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region). Each of these geographical areas is treated as an administrative area with different levels of local governance applying to each.

[Liverpool City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council) is the governing body solely for the Metropolitan Borough of Liverpool and performs functions that are standard of an English [Unitary Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_Authority). The [Liverpool City Region Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region_Combined_Authority) and the [Mayor of the Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_Liverpool_City_Region) reserve major strategic powers over such things as transport, economic development and regeneration for the city along with the 5 surrounding boroughs of the Liverpool City Region. The Combined Authority has competency over areas which have been devolved by national government and are specific to the city region.[^cite_note-138]

Nevertheless, there are a few exceptions to local governance apart from these two structures. Liverpool was administered by [Merseyside County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside_County_Council) between 1974 and 1986 and some residual aspects of organisation which date back to this time have survived. When the County Council was disbanded in 1986, most civic functions were transferred to Liverpool City Council. However, several authorities such as the [police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside_Police) and [fire and rescue service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside_Fire_and_Rescue_Service), continue to be run at a county-wide level. The county of Merseyside, therefore, continues to exist as an administrative area for a few limited services only, while the capability and capacity of the Liverpool City Region Combined Authority is evolving over time.[^cite_note-139]

The city also elects five [members of Parliament](3776360.md) (MPs) to the [Westminster Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_Parliament), all Labour as of the 2024 general election.

### Liverpool City Council

#### City Council Leader and Cabinet



City Councillors meet regularly at the Council Chamber in [Liverpool Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Town_Hall) to conduct civic business.

[^cite_note-140]

Liverpool City Council operates under a constitution comprising 85 city councillors who are directly elected by the Liverpool electorate every four years and represent a variety of different [political parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_the_United_Kingdom). The city councillors make decisions about local services for the city's people.

At each election, the political party that wins the majority of the 85 council seats leads the council for the following four years. The local leader of this party assumes the role of Leader of the City Council who then chairs a Cabinet of 9 councillors who are assigned specific responsibilities known as 'portfolios'.

The incumbent Leader of Liverpool City Council is [Councillor Liam Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liam_Robinson_\(politician\)), who represents the [Labour Party](19279158.md), which secured a large majority at the [2023 local election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Liverpool_City_Council_election),[^cite_note-141] the Leader is also a member of the Mayor of the Liverpool City Regions cabinet (*Separate from the City Council Cabinet*), at present Robinson holds the portfolio of Cabinet Member for Innovation.

The City Council's decisions and scrutiny of activities are undertaken by a number of different committees and panels which include the Overview and Scrutiny Committees, Scrutiny Panels, Regulatory Committees and other committees. The day-to-day management of the council is carried out by the management team which includes the Chief Executive and several directors and senior officers. The management team works with the Cabinet and councillors to deliver strategic direction and priorities such as the budget and the City Plan.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143]

#### Liverpool City Council elections

Main articles: [Liverpool City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council) and [Liverpool City Council elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council_elections)

Every four years, the city elects 85 [councillors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Councillor) from 64 local council [wards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ward_\(politics\)).[^cite_note-144]

During the [2023 Liverpool City Council election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Liverpool_City_Council_election), the [Labour Party](19279158.md) consolidated its control of Liverpool City Council, following on from the previous elections. Out of the total 85 City Council seats up for election, The Labour Party won 61 seats (53.13% of the electorate's total votes), the [Liberal Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Democrats_\(UK\)) won 15 seats (21.61% of the votes), the [Green Party](79787.md) won three seats (9.76% of the votes), the Liverpool Community Independents won three seats (4.64% of the votes) and the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(UK,_1989\)) won the remaining three seats (3.21% of the votes). The [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)), the political party in power at [national government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election), had no representation on Liverpool City Council. Only 27.27% of the eligible Liverpool electorate turned out to vote.[^cite_note-145]



[Steve Rotheram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Rotheram), [Mayor of the Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_Liverpool_City_Region)



[Paula Barker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paula_Barker), MP for [Liverpool Wavertree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Wavertree_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\))



[Ian Byrne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Byrne), MP for [Liverpool West Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_West_Derby_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\))



[Dan Carden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Carden), MP for [Liverpool Walton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Walton_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\))



[Maria Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Eagle), MP for [Liverpool Garston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Garston_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\))



[Kim Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Johnson_\(politician\)), MP for [Liverpool Riverside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Riverside_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\))

Throughout most of the 19th and early 20th centuries, Liverpool was a municipal stronghold of [Toryism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)). However, support for the Conservative Party in recent times has been among the lowest in any part of Britain, particularly since the [monetarist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monetarism) economic policies of former prime minister [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher). After the [1979 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_United_Kingdom_general_election), many have claimed that her victory contributed to longstanding high unemployment and decline in the city.[^cite_note-146] Liverpool is one of the Labour Party's key strongholds; however, the city has also seen hard times under Labour governments. Particularly in the [Winter of Discontent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_of_Discontent) (late 1978 and early 1979) when Liverpool suffered public sector strikes along with the rest of the United Kingdom, but also when it suffered the particularly humiliating misfortune of having [grave-diggers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravedigger) going on strike, leaving the dead unburied for long periods.[^cite_note-147]

#### City Council criticism and improvement

In recent years, [Liverpool City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council) began an extensive improvement program designed to ensure that the authority makes efficient use of [taxpayers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxpayer)' money and to encourage more business and investment in the city. [Grosvenor Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grosvenor_Group), the property company responsible for [Liverpool One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_One), commended the changes as an "opportunity for bold thinking in liverpool".[^cite_note-148]

In 2021, a highly critical government inspection and subsequent report of Liverpool City Council (referred to as the Caller report) identified multiple shortcomings at Liverpool City Council. The [Secretary of State for Housing, Communities and Local Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Housing,_Communities_and_Local_Government), [Robert Jenrick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Jenrick) sent government [commissioners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioner) to oversee the City Council's highways, regeneration, property management, governance and financial decision-making. The authority was compelled to commit to a three-year improvement plan in which the entire structure of the council would be overhauled. As a result of the intervention, major structural changes at the City Council took place by the [2023 United Kingdom local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_United_Kingdom_local_elections), which were labelled "the most unpredictable \[elections] in the city's history". The number of electoral wards in the city was doubled from 30 to 64, while the overall number of city councillors up for election was reduced from 90 to 85. In future, the council would also change to 'all out' elections every four years whereby every single city councillor would be eligible for re-election at the same time. The role of [elected city mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Liverpool) was also abolished and the Council reverted to the previous [Leader and Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_arrangements) style of leadership. The outcome of the elections were seen not only as a test of how the general public would respond to the government intervention in the city, but also to Prime Minister [Rishi Sunak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rishi_Sunak)'s government as a whole.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152]

[Liam Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liam_Robinson_\(politician\)) became the new Leader of Liverpool City Council at the 2023 City Council election. The Liverpool Strategic Futures Advisory Panel, chaired by the [Mayor of Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Mayor_of_the_Liverpool_City_Region) [Steve Rotheram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Rotheram), and including several high-profile figures with experience in [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_England), was established. The panel was tasked with directing the council's long-term future outside of government intervention measures and to advise on plans and priorities that the city should pursue.[^cite_note-153]



[Liverpool Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Town_Hall) houses the official office for the [Lord Mayor of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Mayor_of_Liverpool)

In February 2008, Liverpool City Council was reported to be the worst-performing council in the country, receiving just a one-star rating (classified as inadequate). The main cause of the poor rating was attributed to the council's poor handling of tax-payer money, including the accumulation of a £20 million shortfall while the city held the title of [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture).[^cite_note-154] In April 2024, the [Office for Local Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_for_Levelling_Up,_Housing_and_Communities) released a ranking of local authorities, placing Liverpool City Council 317th out of a possible 318.[^cite_note-155]

#### Lord Mayor of Liverpool

Not to be confused with elected [Mayor of the Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_Liverpool_City_Region) See below.

The [Lord Mayor of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Mayor_of_Liverpool) is an ancient ceremonial role. Councillors within Liverpool City Council (not the general public) elect the Lord Mayor annually, who then serves a one-year term. The Lord Mayor is styled as the "first citizen" and is chosen to represent the city at civic functions and engagements, promote it to the wider world, support local charities and community groups, attend religious events, meet delegates from [Liverpool's twin cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_city), chair council meetings and confer [Honorary Freemen and associations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_the_City).[^cite_note-156] The Lord Mayor is also the presiding officer for Liverpool City Council full meetings.

### Mayor of the Liverpool City Region



[1 Mann Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mann_Island_Buildings) contains the offices of the [Mayor of the Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_the_Liverpool_City_Region) and combined authority.

Liverpool is one of the six constituent boroughs of the [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region). The [Mayor of the Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Mayor_of_the_Liverpool_City_Region) is directly every four years by residents of those six boroughs and oversees the [Liverpool City Region Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region_Combined_Authority). The Combined Authority is the top-tier administrative body for the local governance of the city region and is tasked with taking major strategic decisions on issues such as transport and investment, economic development, employment and skills, tourism, culture, housing and physical infrastructure. The current Mayor is [Steve Rotheram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Rotheram).

### Parliamentary constituencies and MPs

See also: [List of Parliamentary constituencies on Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Parliamentary_constituencies_on_Merseyside)

Liverpool is included within five [parliamentary constituencies](1784582.md), through which MPs are elected to represent the city in [Westminster](13964.md): [Liverpool Garston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Garston_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\)), [Liverpool Riverside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Riverside_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\)), [Liverpool Walton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Walton_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\)), [Liverpool Wavertree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Wavertree_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\)) and [Liverpool West Derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_West_Derby_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\)).[^cite_note-157] At the [last general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_United_Kingdom_general_election), all were won by Labour with representation being from [Maria Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Eagle), [Kim Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Johnson_\(politician\)), [Dan Carden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Carden), [Paula Barker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paula_Barker) and [Ian Byrne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Byrne_\(politician\)) respectively.[^cite_note-2019_general_election_results-158]

## Geography

### Environment



[Satellite imagery](1184557.md) showing [Liverpool Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Bay), Liverpool and the wider [Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside) area

Liverpool has been described as having "the most splendid setting of any English city".[^cite_note-159] At [53°24′0″N 2°59′0″W  /  53.40000°N 2.98333°W  / 53.40000; -2.98333](https://geohack.toolforge.org/geohack.php?pagename=Liverpool\&params=53_24_0_N_2_59_0_W_type\:city) (53.4, −2.98), 176 miles (283 kilometres) north-west of London, located on the [Liverpool Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Bay) of the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea), Liverpool is built across a ridge of [sandstone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandstone) hills rising up to a height of around 230 feet (70 m) above sea-level at Everton Hill, which represents the southern boundary of the [West Lancashire Coastal Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Lancashire_Coastal_Plain).

The [Mersey Estuary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mersey_Estuary) separates Liverpool from the [Wirral Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirral_Peninsula). The boundaries of Liverpool are adjacent to [Bootle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bootle), [Crosby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crosby,_Merseyside) and [Maghull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghull) in south [Sefton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Sefton) to the north, and [Kirkby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirkby), [Huyton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huyton), [Prescot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prescot) and [Halewood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halewood) in [Knowsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Knowsley) to the east.

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Liverpool)

Liverpool experiences a [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate) [maritime climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification): *Cfb*), like much of the British Isles, with relatively mild summers, cool winters and rainfall spread fairly evenly throughout the year. Rainfall and temperature records had been kept at [Bidston Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bidston_Hill) since 1867, but records for atmospheric pressure go back as far as at least 1846.[^cite_note-160] Bidston closed down in 2002 but the [Met Office](319671.md) also has a weather station at [Crosby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crosby,_Merseyside). Since records began in 1867, temperatures have ranged from −17.6 °C (0.3 °F) on 21 December 2010 to 34.5 °C (94.1 °F) on 2 August 1990, although [Liverpool Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_John_Lennon_Airport) recorded a temperature of 35.0 °C (95.0 °F) on 19 July 2006.[^cite_note-161]

The lowest amount of sunshine on record was 16.5 hours in December 1927 whereas the most was 314.5 hours in July 2013.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163]

[Tornado](37530.md) activity or [funnel cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funnel_cloud) formation is very rare in and around the Liverpool area and tornadoes that do form are usually weak. Recent tornadoes or funnel clouds in Merseyside have been seen in 1998 and 2014.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165]

During the period 1981–2010, Crosby recorded an average of 32.8 days of air frost per year, which is low for the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-166] Snow is fairly common during the winter although heavy snow is rare. Snow generally falls between November and March but can occasionally fall earlier and later. In recent times, the earliest snowfall was on 1 October 2008[^cite_note-167] while the latest occurred on 15 May 2012.[^cite_note-168] Although historically, the earliest snowfall occurred on 10 September 1908[^cite_note-169] and the latest on 2 June 1975.[^cite_note-170]

Rainfall, although light, is quite a common occurrence in Liverpool, with the wettest month on record being August 1956, which recorded 221.2 mm (8.71 in) of rain and the driest being February 1932, with 0.9 mm (0.035 in).[^cite_note-web.archive.org-171] The driest year on record was 1991, with 480.5 mm (18.92 in) of rainfall and the wettest was 1872, with 1,159.9 mm (45.67 in).[^cite_note-172]

In 2010, Liverpool City Council and the Primary Care Trust commissioned the [Mersey Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mersey_Forest) to complete "A Green Infrastructure Strategy" for the city.[^cite_note-185]

#### Green belt

Further information: [North West Green Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_Green_Belt)

Liverpool is a core urban element of a [green belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_belt_\(United_Kingdom\)) region that extends into the wider surrounding counties, which is in place to reduce [urban sprawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_sprawl), prevent the towns in the conurbation from further convergence, protect the identity of outlying communities, encourage [brownfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brownfield) reuse, and preserve nearby countryside. This is achieved by restricting inappropriate development within the designated areas and imposing stricter conditions on permitted building.[^cite_note-186]

Due to being already highly built up, the city contains limited portions of protected green belt area within [greenfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenfield_land) throughout the borough at [Fazakerley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fazakerley), [Croxteth Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croxteth_Hall) and country park and Craven Wood, Woodfields Park and nearby golf courses in [Netherley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherley,_Liverpool), small greenfield tracts east of the [Speke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speke) area by the St Ambrose primary school, and the small hamlet of [Oglet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oglet) and the surrounding area south of [Liverpool Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_John_Lennon_Airport).[^cite_note-belt2-187]

The green belt was first drawn up in 1983 under Merseyside County Council[^cite_note-188] and the size in the city amounts to 530 hectares (5.3 km<sup>2</sup>; 2.0 mi<sup>2</sup>).[^cite_note-189]

## Demonyms

### Scouser

"Scousers" redirects here. For the Harry Enfield sketch, see [The Scousers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Scousers).

Since the mid-20th century, Scouser has become the predominant demonym for the inhabitants of Liverpool, and is strongly associated with the [Scouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scouse) accent and dialect of the city.[^cite_note-JohnBelchamMerseyPride-190] The Scouse accent is described as progressively diverging from the Lancastrian accent in the late 19th century.[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194][^cite_note-TonyCrowley-195]

The etymology of Scouser is derived from the traditional dish [Scouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobscouse) brought to the area by sailors travelling through Liverpool's port.[^cite_note-TonyCrowley-195][^cite_note-196][^cite_note-TonyCrowleyBook-197]

### Earlier demonyms

Earlier terms used to refer to inhabitants of Liverpool include:

- Liverpoldon (17th century)

  [^cite_note-198]

- Leeirpooltonian (17th century)

  [^cite_note-TonyCrowley-195]

- Liverpolitan (19th century)

  [^cite_note-199]

- Liverpudlian (19th century to present)

  [^cite_note-200]

Professor Tony Crowley writes that up until the 1950s, inhabitants of Liverpool were generally referred to by a number of demonyms, including, in the mid-20th century, the rival terms of 'Liverpolitan' and 'Liverpudlian'. Debate surrounded the lexicology of these terms and their connotations of social class.[^cite_note-TonyCrowleyBook-197][^cite_note-TonyCrowleyEcho1-201]

Professor John Belchem suggests that a series of other nicknames such as 'Dick Liver', 'Dicky Sam' and 'whacker' were used, but gradually fell out of use. Belchem and Philip Boland suggest that comedic radio presenters and entertainers brought the Liverpool identity to a national audience, which in turn encouraged locals to be gradually more known as 'scousers'. By the time that Frank Shaw's *My Liverpool, a Celebration of 'Scousetown'* was published in 1971, Belchem argues, 'Scouser' had firmly become the dominant demonym.[^cite_note-JohnBelchamMerseyPride-190][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203]

## Demography

Main article: [Demographics of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Liverpool)

### Population

| Date | Population | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **1207** |  | Borough of Liverpool founded by [John, King of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John,_King_of_England). The economy was focused on agricultural and food processing, [grain mills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gristmill) and warehouses until the 16th century. |
| **1272** | 840 |  |
| **14th century** | 1,000 – 1,200 | Population roughly 1,000 in 1300. Because Liverpool was a port, it was more at risk from the spread of disease. Townspeople lived partly by farming and fishing. Some were craftsmen or tradesmen such as bakers, brewers, butchers, blacksmiths, and carpenters. A [watermill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watermill) existed to ground grain into flour for the townspeople's bread, and there was a windmill. [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) wiped out whole families and bodies were buried in a mass grave at [St Nicholas's churchyard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Our_Lady_and_Saint_Nicholas,_Liverpool). |
| **16th century** |  | Ireland was still Liverpool's main trading partner. In 1540, a writer said: "Irish merchants come much hither as to a good harbor". He also said there was "good merchandise at Liverpool and much Irish [yarn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarn), that [Manchester](20206.md) men buy there". Skins and hides were still imported from Ireland. Exports from Liverpool included coal, woolen cloth, knives and leather goods. There were still many fishermen in Liverpool. In the mid 16th century, the town was under the control of the country [gentry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentry) and trade was slow. The population dropped to below 600, in part due to deaths in the [1558 plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1557_influenza_pandemic) when a third of the townspeople died. Further [plague outbreaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_plague_pandemic) took place in 1609, 1647 and 1650 which led to static or retrogressive population levels. The town was regarded as subordinate to [Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester) until the 1650s. |
| **1600** | <2,000 | English troops bound for [rebellions in Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Irish_uprisings) settled in the 16th and early 17th centuries. |
| **1626** |  | [Charles I of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_I_of_England) issued new [Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_charter) for the town. Trade with other cities, Ireland, [Isle of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Man), France and Spain increased. Fish and wool was exported to the [Continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe), and wines, iron and other commodities imported. In the following decades, merchants invested in Liverpool and its importance grew. Regular shipping began to America and [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Indies). Liverpool was controlled by [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown), the Molyneux and Stanley families. |
| **1642** | 2,500 | Liverpool overtook [Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester) in exporting coal and salt in early 17th century, especially to Ireland. |
| **1644** |  | During [English Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Civil_War), [Prince Rupert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Rupert_of_the_Rhine) led a [royalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cavalier) army to capture Liverpool. He described the town as a "mere crow's nest which a parcel of boys could take". He stormed [Liverpool Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Castle) in the 'Siege of Liverpool' with considerable slaughter. |
| **1647** |  | Liverpool was made a free and independent port, no longer subject to [Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester). |
| **1648** |  | First recorded cargo from America landed at Liverpool. |
| **Late 17th century** |  | Liverpool grew rapidly with the growth of [English colonies in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_history_of_the_United_States) and [West Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_West_Indies). Liverpool was well placed to trade across Atlantic Ocean. The writer [Celia Fiennes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celia_Fiennes) visited Liverpool and said: "Liverpool is built on the [River Mersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Mersey). It is mostly newly built, of brick and stone after the London fashion. The original (town) was a few fishermen's houses. It has now grown into a large, fine town. It is but one parish with one church though there be 24 streets in it, there is indeed a little chapel and there are a great many dissenters in the town ([Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestants) who did not belong to the [Church of England](5955.md)). It's a very rich trading town, the houses are of brick and stone, built high and even so that a street looks very handsome. The streets are well paved. There is an abundance of persons who are well dressed and fashionable. The streets are fair and long. It's London in miniature as much as I ever saw anything. There is a very pretty exchange. It stands on 8 pillars, over which is a very handsome [Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Town_Hall)." |
| **1700** | 5,714 | First recorded Liverpool [slave ship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_ship), the 'Liverpool Merchant', sold a cargo of 220 [slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave) in Barbados. In the early 1700s, the writer [Daniel Defoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Defoe) said: "Liverpool has an opulent, flourishing and increasing trade to [Virginia](32432.md) and English colonies in America. They trade around the whole island (of Great Britain), send ships to [Norway](21241.md), to [Hamburg](13467.md), and to the [Baltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states) as also to [Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holland) and [Flanders](10878.md) (roughly modern [Belgium](3343.md))." [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_people) people in search of work and opportunity made up a large amount of population in the early 18th century. |
| **1715** |  | [World's first wet dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Dock) opened in Liverpool, symbolising a new era in the town's growth, the starting point of the 18th-century boom in Liverpool's fortunes. |
| **1720s** |  | [Liverpool Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Castle) demolished (built in the 1230s) |

Liverpool was second only to London in importance. Poor, overcrowded and unsanitary living conditions led to disease and epidemics of [cholera](7591.md) in 1830s to 1860s. |
