#### The city

The city of Liverpool is at the core of a much larger and more populous [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_area); however, at the most recent [UK Census in 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_census), the area governed by [Liverpool City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council) had a population of 486,100, a 4.2% increase from the previous [Census in 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_United_Kingdom_census). This figure increased to 500,500 people by 2022, according to data from Liverpool City Council.

Taking in to account how [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_England) is organised within the cities and metropolitan areas of England, the Liverpool was the fifth largest of England's ['core cities'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Core_Cities_Group) and had the second-overall-highest population density of those, by 2021.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216]

The population of the city has steadily risen since the [2001 Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_United_Kingdom_census). As well as having a growing population, the population density also grew at the 2021 Census compared to the previous census. Since 2011, its population size ranked 10th out of 309 local authority areas.[^cite_note-217]

The population of the city is comparatively younger than that of England as a whole. Family life in the city is also growing at odds with the North West England region as a whole: At the 2021 Census, the percentage of households including a couple without children increased in Liverpool, but fell across the North West. The percentage of people aged 16 years and over (excluding full-time students) who were employed also increased in Liverpool compared to the overall North West region where it fell.&#x20;



Liverpool's [ethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_the_United_Kingdom) and international population is growing. More people in the city identified as [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Asians) and [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_British_people) in the [most recent census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_census), compared to the previous census.

The 2021 Census also showed that Liverpool's [ethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_the_United_Kingdom) and international population was growing. The number of residents in the city born outside of England has increased since the previous census, while the number of residents who did not identify with any [national identity associated with the UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom) has also increased at a faster rate than England as a whole. The overall share of the city's population who identified as [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Asians) and [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_British_people) increased, while the percentage who identified as [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_people_in_the_United_Kingdom) decreased in the city compared with previous Census.[^cite_note-2021lifechange-218]

It has been argued that the city can claim to have one of the strongest [Irish heritages](775859.md) in the United Kingdom, with as many as 75 per cent (estimated) of Liverpool's population with some form of [Irish ancestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_people_in_Great_Britain).[^cite_note-219]

The growing population of Liverpool in the 21st century reverses a trend which took place between the 1930s and 2001, when the population of the [city proper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_proper) effectively halved.

At the [1931 United Kingdom census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1931_United_Kingdom_census), Liverpool's population reached an all-time high of 846,302. Following this peak, in response to central government policy, the Council authority of Liverpool then built and owned large several ['new town'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_towns_in_the_United_Kingdom) [council estates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_housing_in_the_United_Kingdom) in the suburbs within Liverpool's metropolitan area. Tens of thousands of people were systematically relocated to new housing in areas such as [Halton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Halton), [Knowsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Knowsley), [St Helens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_St_Helens), [Sefton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Sefton), [Wirral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Wirral), [Cheshire West and Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheshire_West_and_Chester), [West Lancashire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Lancashire), [Warrington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrington) and as far as [North Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Wales).

Such a mass relocation and population loss during this time was common practice for many British cities, including London and [Manchester](20206.md), In contrast, satellite towns such as [Kirkby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirkby), [Skelmersdale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skelmersdale) and [Runcorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runcorn) saw a corresponding rise in their populations (Kirkby being the fastest growing town in Britain during the 1960s).[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221][^cite_note-2021lifechange-218][^cite_note-222]

#### Urban and metropolitan area

Liverpool is typically grouped with the wider [Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside) (plus [Halton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Halton)) area for the purpose of defining its metropolitan footprint, and there are several methodologies. Sometimes, this metropolitan area is broadened to encompass urban settlements in the neighbouring counties of [Lancashire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancashire) and [Cheshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheshire).[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224]

The [Office for National Statistics](324029.md) in the United Kingdom uses the international standardised [International Territorial Levels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Territorial_Level) (ITLs) to divide up the [economic territory of the UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_United_Kingdom). This enables the ONS to calculate regional and local statistics and data. The ONS uses a series of codes to identify these areas. In order of hierarchy from largest area to smallest area, Liverpool is part of the following regions:[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227]

##### ITL 1 region

[North West England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_England) (code TLD) \
At the 2021 Census, the ITL 1 region of North West England had a usual resident population of 7,417,300.[^cite_note-228]

##### ITL 2 region

[Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside) (code TLD7) \
The ITL 2 region of Merseyside is defined as the area comprising East Merseyside (TLD71) plus Liverpool (TLD72), [Sefton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Sefton) (TLD73) and [Wirral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Wirral) (TLD74). \
At the 2021 Census, the population of this area was as follows:[^cite_note-229]

East Merseyside (TLD71):

- [Halton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Halton) = 128,200

- [Knowsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Knowsley) = 154,500

- [St. Helens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_St_Helens) = 183,200

Liverpool (TLD72) = 486,100

[Sefton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Sefton) (TLD73) = 279,300

[Wirral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Wirral) (TLD74) = 320,200

Therefore, the total population of the ITL 2 Merseyside region was 1,551,500 based on the 2021 Census.

##### ITL 3 region

The smallest ITL 3 area classed as Liverpool (code TLD72), therefore, had a population of 486,100 at the 2021 Census.

##### Other definitions

At the 2021 Census, the ONS used a refreshed concept of built-up areas (BUAs) based on the physical built environment, using [satellite imagery](1184557.md) to recognise developed land, such as cities, towns and villages. This allows the ONS to investigate economic and social statistics based on actual settlements where most people live. Data from the 2021 Census is not directly comparable with [2011 Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_United_Kingdom_census) data due to this revised methodology. Using the population figures of BUAs at the 2021 Census (excluding London), [Liverpool Built-up Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Built-up_Area) is the third largest in England with some 506,565 usual residents (behind only [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham) and [Leeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leeds)). Liverpool's built-up area is, therefore, larger than the major English cities of [Bristol](36741.md), Manchester, [Newcastle upon Tyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newcastle_upon_Tyne), [Nottingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottingham) and [Sheffield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheffield).[^cite_note-230]



Map showing the six boroughs of [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region): the fourth-largest [combined authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_authority) area in England.

Excluding London, the [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region) was the fourth-largest [combined authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_authority) area in England, by 2021. The population is approximately 1.6 million. The Liverpool City Region is a political and economic partnership between local authorities including [Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council), plus the [Metropolitan boroughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_borough) of [Knowsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Knowsley), [Sefton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Sefton), [St Helens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_St_Helens), [Wirral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Wirral) and the [Borough of Halton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Halton). The [Liverpool City Region Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region_Combined_Authority) exercises strategic governance powers for the region in many areas. The economic data of the Liverpool city region is of particular policy interest to the [Office for National Statistics](324029.md), particularly as the [British Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Government) continuously explores the potential to negotiate increased [devolved powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom) for each combined authority area.[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234]

A 2011 report, *Liverpool City Region – Building on its Strengths*, by [Michael Heseltine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Heseltine) and [Terry Leahy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry_Leahy), stated that "what is now called Liverpool City Region has a population of around 1.5 million", but also referred to "an urban region that spreads from [Wrexham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wrexham) and [Flintshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flintshire) to [Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester), [Warrington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrington), [West Lancashire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Lancashire) and across to [Southport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southport)", with a population of 2.3 million.[^cite_note-235]

In 2006, in an attempt to harmonise the series of metropolitan areas across the European Union, ESPON (now [European Observation Network for Territorial Development and Cohesion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Observation_Network_for_Territorial_Development_and_Cohesion)) released a study defining a "[Liverpool/Birkenhead Metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPON_metropolitan_areas_in_the_United_Kingdom)" with an estimated population of 2.241 million people. The metro area comprised a functional urban area consisting of a contiguous urban sprawl, labour pool, and commuter [Travel to work areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_to_work_area). The analysis defined this metropolitan area as Liverpool itself, combined with the surrounding areas of [Birkenhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkenhead), [Wigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wigan)/[Ashton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashton-in-Makerfield), [Warrington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrington), [Widnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widnes)/[Runcorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runcorn), [Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester), [Southport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southport), [Ellesmere Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellesmere_Port), [Ormskirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ormskirk) and [Skelmersdale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skelmersdale).[^cite_note-ESPON_project-236]

Liverpool and Manchester are sometimes considered as one large polynuclear metropolitan area,[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239] or [megalopolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megalopolis_\(city_type\)).

### Ethnicity

In recent decades, Liverpool's population is becoming more multicultural. According to the 2021 census, 77% of all Liverpool residents described their ethnic group as White English, Welsh, Scottish, Northern Irish or British. The remaining 23% were described as non-White English/British. Between 2011 and 2021, there was population growth across all ethnic groups, except 'White English/British' and 'Any Other', where there were overall losses. The number of 'Other White residents' in Liverpool also increased by almost 12,000 people, with notable increases in the 'Other Asian', 'Arab', and 'Other Mixed/Multiple' population categories. The non-White English/British population as a percentage of the total population across the ['newly organised city electoral wards'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Liverpool_City_Council_election) ranged from 5% in the [Orrell Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orrell_Park_\(Liverpool_ward\)) ward to 69% in the [Princes Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princes_Park_\(Liverpool_ward\)) ward. Nine out of ten Liverpool residents regarded English as their main language. The most spoken non-English languages in the city were [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) (5,743 main speakers) followed by [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language) (4,809 main speakers). Overall, almost 45,000 residents had a main language that was not English.[^cite_note-2021ethnicity-240]&#x20;

According to a 2014 survey, the ten most popular surnames of Liverpool and their occurrence in the population are:[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243]

1\. Jones – 23,012

2\. Smith – 16,276

3\. Williams – 13,997

4\. Davies – 10,149

5\. Hughes – 9,787

6\. Roberts – 9,571

7\. Taylor – 8,219

8\. Johnson – 6,715

9\. Brown – 6,603

10\. Murphy – 6,495

Liverpool is home to [the UK's oldest black community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people_in_Liverpool), dating back to at least the 1730s. Some Liverpudlians can trace their black ancestry in the city back ten generations.[^cite_note-244] Early Black settlers in the city included seamen, the children of traders sent to be educated, and [freed slaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_slave_trade), since slaves entering the country after 1722 were deemed free men.[^cite_note-McIntyre-Brown-245] Since the 20th century, Liverpool is also noted for its large [African-Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_African-Caribbean_people),[^cite_note-2021_Nomis-5] [Ghanaian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghanaians_in_the_United_Kingdom),[^cite_note-IOMGhanaians-246] and [Somali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somalis_in_the_United_Kingdom)[^cite_note-247] communities, formed of more recent African-descended immigrants and their subsequent generations.



Liverpool has the oldest [Chinese community](1255834.md) in Europe and the largest [Chinese arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paifang) outside China.

The city is also home to the oldest [Chinese community](1255834.md) in Europe; the first residents of the city's [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Liverpool) arrived as seamen in the 19th century.[^cite_note-Chinese-248] The traditional Chinese gateway erected in Liverpool's Chinatown is the largest such gateway outside China. Liverpool also has a long-standing [Filipino](2298105.md) community. [Lita Roza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lita_Roza), a singer from Liverpool who was the first woman to achieve a UK number one hit, had Filipino ancestry.

The city is also known for its large [Irish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_people_in_Great_Britain) and [Welsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_people) populations.[^cite_note-IrishWelsh-249] In 1813, 10 per cent of Liverpool's population was Welsh, leading to the city becoming known as "the capital of [North Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Wales)."[^cite_note-IrishWelsh-249]

During, and in the decades following, the [Great Irish Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Irish_Famine) in the mid-19th century, up to two million Irish people travelled to Liverpool within one decade, with many subsequently departing for the United States.[^cite_note-250] By 1851, more than 20 per cent of the population of Liverpool was Irish.[^cite_note-251] At the 2001 Census, 1.17 per cent of the population were Welsh-born and 0.75 per cent were born in the [Republic of Ireland](14560.md), while 0.54 per cent were born in [Northern Ireland](21265.md),[^cite_note-CoB-252] but many more Liverpudlians are of legacy Welsh or Irish ancestry.[^cite_note-253]

Other contemporary ethnicities include [Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Indian),[^cite_note-2021_Nomis-5] [Latin American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_American_migration_to_the_United_Kingdom),[^cite_note-Latin_American_Liverpool-254] [Malaysian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysians_in_the_United_Kingdom),[^cite_note-IOMMalaysians-255] and [Yemeni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemenis_in_the_United_Kingdom)[^cite_note-BBC_Yemenis-256] communities, which number several thousand each.

[Liverpool Anglican Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cathedral), one of the largest cathedrals in the world



[Liverpool Metropolitan Cathedral of Christ the King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Metropolitan_Cathedral)



The [Al-Rahma Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Rahma_Mosque) in the [Toxteth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toxteth) area of Liverpool



[Princes Road Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princes_Road_Synagogue), Toxteth

The thousands of migrants and sailors passing through Liverpool resulted in a religious diversity that is still apparent today. This is reflected in the equally diverse collection of religious buildings,[^cite_note-258] including two Christian cathedrals.

Liverpool is known to be England's 'most Catholic city', with a [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic) population much larger than in other parts of England.[^cite_note-Sectarianism_in_England-259] This is mainly due to high historic Irish migration to the city and their descendants since.[^cite_note-260]

The parish church of Liverpool is the Anglican [Our Lady and St Nicholas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Our_Lady_and_Saint_Nicholas,_Liverpool), colloquially known as "the sailors church", which has existed near the waterfront since 1257. It regularly plays host to [Catholic masses](5684685.md). Other notable churches include the [Greek Orthodox Church of St Nicholas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church_of_St_Nicholas) (built in the [Neo-Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Byzantine_architecture) style) and the [Gustav Adolf Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustav_Adolf_Church,_Liverpool) (the Swedish Seamen's Church, reminiscent of Nordic styles).

Liverpool's wealth as a port city enabled the construction of two enormous cathedrals in the 20th century. The [Anglican Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cathedral), which was designed by [Sir Giles Gilbert Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giles_Gilbert_Scott) and plays host to the annual [Liverpool Shakespeare Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Shakespeare_Festival), has one of the longest [naves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nave), largest organs and heaviest and highest peals of bells in the world. The Roman Catholic [Metropolitan Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Metropolitan_Cathedral), on Mount Pleasant next to [Liverpool Science Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Science_Park), was initially planned to be even larger. Of [Sir Edwin Lutyens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Edwin_Lutyens)'s original design, only the crypt was completed. The cathedral was eventually built to a simpler design by [Sir Frederick Gibberd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sir_Frederick_Gibberd). While this is on a smaller scale than Lutyens' original design, it still incorporates the largest panel of [stained glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stained_glass) in the world. The road running between the two cathedrals is called [Hope Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hope_Street,_Liverpool). The cathedral has long been colloquially referred to as "Paddy's Wigwam" due to its shape.[^cite_note-261]

Liverpool contains several synagogues, of which the Grade I listed [Moorish Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moorish_Revival) [Princes Road Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princes_Road_Synagogue) is architecturally the most notable. Princes Road is widely considered to be the most magnificent of Britain's Moorish Revival synagogues and one of the finest buildings in Liverpool.[^cite_note-262] Liverpool has a thriving [Jewish community](25955086.md) with a further two [orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodox_Judaism) Synagogues, one in the [Allerton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allerton,_Liverpool) district of the city and a second in the [Childwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childwall) district of the city where a significant Jewish community reside. A third orthodox Synagogue in the [Greenbank Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenbank_Park) area of L17 has recently closed and is a listed 1930s structure. There is also a [Lubavitch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lubavitch) [Chabad House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chabad_House) and a [reform synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_synagogue). Liverpool has had a Jewish community since the mid-18th century. The Jewish population of Liverpool is around 5,000.[^cite_note-263] The [Liverpool Talmudical College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Talmudical_College) existed from 1914 until 1990, when its classes moved to the [Childwall Synagogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Jewish_communities_in_the_United_Kingdom).

Liverpool also has a [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) community, with a [Mandir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandir) on [Edge Lane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edge_Lane), [Edge Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edge_Hill,_Merseyside). The Shri Radha Krishna Temple from the Hindu Cultural Organisation in Liverpool is located there.[^cite_note-264] Liverpool also has the [Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak) [Sikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh) [Gurdwara](482849.md) in [Wavertree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavertree)[^cite_note-265] and a [Baháʼí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith) Centre in the same area.[^cite_note-266]

The city had the earliest Mosque in England and possibly the UK, founded in 1887 by [William Abdullah Quilliam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Abdullah_Quilliam), a lawyer who had converted to [Islam](6037917.md) who set up the [Liverpool Muslim Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Muslim_Institute) in a [terraced house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terraced_house) on West Derby Road.[^cite_note-267] Apart from the first mosque in England which now houses a museum,[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269] the largest and main one, [Al-Rahma mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Rahma_mosque), was also the third purpose-built mosque in the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-270] The second largest mosque in Liverpool is the Masjid Al-Taiseer.[^cite_note-271] Other mosques in the city include the Bait ul Lateef Ahmadiyya Mosque,[^cite_note-272] Hamza Center (Community Center),[^cite_note-273] Islamic community centre,[^cite_note-274] Liverpool Mosque and Islamic Institute,[^cite_note-275] Liverpool Towhid Centre,[^cite_note-276] Masjid Annour,[^cite_note-277] and the Shah Jalal Mosque.[^cite_note-278]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Liverpool)

See also: [Liverpool city centre § Headquarters and major offices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_city_centre#Headquarters_and_major_offices), [Category\:Companies based in Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Companies_based_in_Liverpool), and [Category\:Manufacturing companies based in Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Manufacturing_companies_based_in_Liverpool)

### City and region



Liverpool is a major component of the third largest regional economy in the United Kingdom. Important sectors in the city include the [knowledge economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_economy), [maritime industry](28769.md), tourism, [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Liverpool), [hospitality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospitality_industry), [healthcare industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_industry), [life sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_life_sciences), the [creative](674483.md) and [digital sectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_economy).

[^cite_note-279]

[^cite_note-280]

[^cite_note-281]



[Liverpool skyline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_and_structures_in_Liverpool) showing the [Commercial District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_city_centre)



Liverpool is one of the top [retail destinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_shopping_centres_in_the_United_Kingdom_by_size) in the UK



[Knowledge Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_Quarter,_Liverpool), Liverpool hosts globally significant institutions



The [Range Rover Evoque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Range_Rover_Evoque) is manufactured at [Jaguar Land Rover Halewood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaguar_Land_Rover_Halewood)



The [Port of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Liverpool) is the [4th largest port in the UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ports_in_Great_Britain)



Liverpool is home to many [global headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_city_centre) and major branch offices

Liverpool forms an integral part of [North West England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_West_England)'s economy, the third largest regional economy in the United Kingdom. The city is also a major contributor to the economy of [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region), worth over £40 billion per year.[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284]

The local authority area governed by [Liverpool City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council) accounts for 39% of the Liverpool city region's total jobs, 40% of its total GVA and 35% of its total businesses. At the local authority level, the city's [GVA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_value_added) (balanced) at current basic prices was £14.3 billion in 2021. Its GDP at current market prices was £15.9 billion. This equates to £32,841 per head of the population.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286]

At the [2021 United Kingdom census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Kingdom_census), 51.1% of Liverpool's population aged 16 years and over was classed as employed, 44.2% economically inactive and 4.8% unemployed. Of those employed, the most popular industries providing the employment were human health and social work activities (18.7%), wholesale and retail trade; repair of motor vehicles and motor cycles (15%), education (10.8%), public administration and defence; compulsory social security (7.3%), accommodation and food service activities (6.8%), construction (6.5%), transport and storage (5.8%), manufacturing (5.5%) and professional, scientific and technical activities (5.2%).[^cite_note-287]

According to the [ONS](324029.md) Business Register and Employment Survey 2021, some industries within Liverpool perform strongly compared to other [local authorities in Great Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_the_United_Kingdom). In terms of absolute number of jobs per industry in Great Britain's local authority areas, Liverpool features in the national top 10 for human health and social work activities; arts, entertainment and recreation; public administration and defence; compulsory social security; accommodation and food service activities and real estate activities. Liverpool features in the national top 20 for number of jobs in education; construction; wholesale and retail trade; repair of motor vehicles and motorcycles; transportation and storage; financial and insurance activities and professional, scientific and technical activities.[^cite_note-Labour_Market_Profile_-_Liverpool-288]

In 2023, Liverpool City Council set out an economic growth plan for the city over the following 20 years. The City Council will have particular focus on economic sectors such as the [visitor economy (tourism)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_England), [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Liverpool), [life sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_life_sciences), [digital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_economy) and [creative sectors](674483.md), and [advanced car manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automotive_industry_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-289]

According to the International passenger Survey, from the [ONS](324029.md), Liverpool was one of the top 5 most visited cities in the UK by overseas tourists in 2022. As of the same year, the city's tourist industry was worth a total of £3.5 billion annually and was part of a larger city region tourist industry worth £5 billion. A consistent calendar of major events, as well as a plethora of cultural attractions, continue to provide a significant draw for tourists. Tourism related to [the Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles) is worth an estimated £100 million to the Liverpool economy each year alone. [Liverpool One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_One), as well as a growing retail offer overall, has led to the city being one of the most prominent destinations for shopping in the UK. [Liverpool Cruise Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cruise_Terminal), which is situated close to the [Pier Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pier_Head), enables tourists to berth in the centre of the city.[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296]

Liverpool is home to the [Knowledge Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_Quarter,_Liverpool), a 450-acre city centre district that hosts some of the world's most influential institutions in science, health, technology, education, music and the creative performing arts. The [UK government](25318118.md) has also identified the city as a '[pharmaceutical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_industry_in_the_United_Kingdom) production superpower' and one of the UK's leading regions for [bioprocessing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomanufacturing). The accolade led to the government choosing the city for England's second ever 'Investment Zone' in 2023. This will involve millions of pounds being invested over the coming years in to science orientated districts including the Knowledge Quarter and the so-called 'pharma cluster' in the city suburb of [Speke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speke). The two clusters form an internationally significant role in [infectious disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infectious_diseases_\(medical_specialty\)) control. Liverpool City Council also plan to invest in the city's [Baltic Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Triangle), which is renowned in the creative and digital industries.[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300][^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302]

[Car manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automotive_industry_in_the_United_Kingdom) also takes place in the city at the [Jaguar Land Rover Halewood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaguar_Land_Rover_Halewood) plant, where the [Range Rover Evoque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Range_Rover_Evoque) model is assembled. In 2023, Jaguar Land Rover announced that the Halewood plant would begin to shift its focus to [electric car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_car) production.[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304]

Historically, the economy of Liverpool was centred on the city's port and manufacturing base. Today, the [Port of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Liverpool) is the UK's fourth-largest port by [tonnage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonnage) of [freight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cargo), handling over 30 million tonnes in 2020. The city is also the UK's largest port for [transatlantic trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatlantic_crossing), handling 45% of the country's trade from the United States. In 2023, the city was chosen by the British government to be a designated [Freeport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_ports_in_the_United_Kingdom) to encourage growing international commerce.

The [Liverpool2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool2) container terminal, completed in 2022, has greatly increased the volume of cargo which Liverpool is able to handle and has facilitated the world's biggest [container vessels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Container_vessel).[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311]

Liverpool is also home to numerous UK headquarters, or the major strategic branch offices, of many [shipping and freight lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipping_line) including: [Atlantic Container Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Container_Line),[^cite_note-312] [Bibby Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibby_Line),[^cite_note-313] Borchard Lines Ltd,[^cite_note-314] [CMA CGM](1903569.md),[^cite_note-315] [Hapag-Lloyd](293818.md),[^cite_note-316] Independent Container Line,[^cite_note-317] [Irish Ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Ferries),[^cite_note-318] [Maersk Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maersk_Line),[^cite_note-319] [Mediterranean Shipping Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Shipping_Company)[^cite_note-320] and [Zim Integrated Shipping Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZIM_\(shipping_company\)).[^cite_note-321]

Liverpool's rich [architectural base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Liverpool) has helped the city become the second most [filmed city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film_industry) in the UK outside London. As well as being a featured location in its own right, it often doubles up for Chicago, London, Moscow, New York City, Paris and [Rome](25458.md). The Depot studios, close to the city centre, provide space for film and TV productions.[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323]

Major economic projects planned for the city include the revitalisation of disused land in the North docks/[Ten Streets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_Streets) area, [Liverpool Waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Waters) and a new purpose-built TV studio at the former [Littlewoods Pools building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Littlewoods_Pools_building), adjacent to the Depot.[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326]

### City region economy and devolution

The [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region) GDP in 2021 was £40.479 billion. The six contributing boroughs to this GDP were as follows:

[^cite_note-327]

1. [Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Council)\
   (£15.911 billion) (39.3%)

2. [Wirral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Wirral)\
   (£6.632 billion) (16.4%)

3. [Sefton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Sefton)\
   (£5.431 billion) (13.4%)

4. [Knowsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Knowsley)\
   (£4.557 billion) (11.3%)

5. [Halton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borough_of_Halton)\
   (£4.498 billion) (11.1%)

6. [St Helens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_St_Helens)\
   (£3.448 billion) (8.52%)

The policy agenda of the [British Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Government) is to continuously monitor the economy and productivity of the UK's [core cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Core_Cities_Group) within the context of their respective [city regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_region). The government's longer-term plan is to assess each area's potential for increased [devolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom) and transfer of additional powers and budgets from central government in [Whitehall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitehall) to their corresponding [combined authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_authorities). As such, official statistics about Liverpool's economy within the context of the [Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region), are closely monitored by the [Office for National Statistics](324029.md). This allows policy and decision makers to more accurately assess the 'functional economic area' of the city, which is not bound by traditional [local government geographies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_England).[^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332]

As of 2023, there are ten city regions in England with combined authorities. The economy of [Liverpool's combined authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region_Combined_Authority) area in comparison to the other city regions is as follows:

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

| [Greater Manchester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Manchester_Combined_Authority) | [Manchester](20206.md) | 78.744 | 87.703 | 30,576 |

## Landmarks

Main articles: [Architecture of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Liverpool) and [Liverpool Maritime Mercantile City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Maritime_Mercantile_City)

See also: [List of tallest buildings and structures in Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_and_structures_in_Liverpool) and [List of public art in Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_public_art_in_Liverpool)



Liverpool's Three Graces, the [Royal Liver Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Liver_Building), [Cunard Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cunard_Building) and [Port of Liverpool Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Liverpool_Building) at the [Pier Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pier_Head)

Liverpool's long commercial history has given rise to a considerable variety of [architectural styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_style) found within the city, ranging from 16th-century [Tudor buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudor_architecture) to modern-day [contemporary architecture](3970948.md).[^cite_note-Hughes_(1999),_p10-334] The majority of buildings in the city date from the late 18th century onwards, the period during which the city grew into one of the foremost powers in the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire).[^cite_note-Hughes_(1999),_p11-335] There are over [2,500 listed buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Listed_buildings_in_Liverpool) in Liverpool, of which 27 are Grade I listed[^cite_note-336] and 85 are Grade II\* listed.[^cite_note-337] The city also has a greater number of public sculptures than any other location in the United Kingdom aside from [Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Westminster) and significant number of Georgian houses still exist.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339] This richness of architecture has subsequently seen Liverpool described by [English Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Heritage), as England's finest Victorian city.[^cite_note-340]

The value of Liverpool's architecture and design was recognised in 2004, when several areas throughout the city were declared a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Site](44940.md). Known as the [Liverpool Maritime Mercantile City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Maritime_Mercantile_City), the sites were added in recognition of the city's role in the development of [international trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_trade) and [docking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dock_\(maritime\)) technology.[^cite_note-341] However, this status was revoked in July 2021, when UNESCO resolved that recent and proposed developments, such as the [Bramley-Moore Dock Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bramley-Moore_Dock_Stadium) and [Liverpool Waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Waters) projects, had resulted in the "serious deterioration" of the area's significance.[^cite_note-342]

### Waterfront and docks



The Liverpool Waterfront with the [Port of Liverpool Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Liverpool_Building), [Museum of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Liverpool), [Royal Albert Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Dock,_Liverpool) and [Wheel of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheel_of_Liverpool) all visible



Modern office and commercial developments on the Liverpool Waterfront

As a major British port, the docks in Liverpool have historically been central to the city's development. Several major docking firsts have occurred in the city including the construction of the world's first enclosed [wet dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_dock) (the [Old Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Dock)) in 1715 and the first ever [hydraulic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydraulic) lifting [cranes](318378.md).[^cite_note-343] The best-known dock in Liverpool is the [Royal Albert Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Dock,_Liverpool), which was constructed in 1846 and today comprises the largest single collection of Grade I listed buildings anywhere in Britain.[^cite_note-344] Built under the guidance of [Jesse Hartley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Hartley), it was considered to be one of the most advanced docks anywhere in the world upon completion and is often attributed with helping the city to become one of the most important ports in the world. Today, the Royal Albert Dock houses restaurants, bars, shops, two hotels as well as the [Merseyside Maritime Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside_Maritime_Museum), [International Slavery Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Slavery_Museum), [Tate Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tate_Liverpool) and [The Beatles Story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles_Story). North of the city centre is [Stanley Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Dock), home to the [Stanley Dock Tobacco Warehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Dock_Tobacco_Warehouse), which was at the time of its construction in 1901, the world's largest building in terms of area[^cite_note-Nicholls,_p38-345] and today stands as the world's largest brick-work building.[^cite_note-346]

One of the most famous locations in Liverpool is the [Pier Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pier_Head), renowned for the trio of buildings—the [Royal Liver Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Liver_Building), the [Cunard Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cunard_Building) and the [Port of Liverpool Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Liverpool_Building)—which sit upon it. Collectively referred to as the *Three Graces*, these buildings stand as a testament to the great wealth in the city during the late 19th and early 20th centuries. Built in a variety of architectural styles, they are recognised as being the symbol of 'maritime Liverpool' and are regarded by many as contributing to one of the most impressive waterfronts in the world.[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-348][^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350]

In the 21st century, several areas along Liverpool's waterfront have undergone significant redevelopment. Among the notable developments are the [Museum of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Liverpool), the construction of the [Liverpool Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Arena), [ACC Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACC_Liverpool) and [Exhibition Centre Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exhibition_Centre_Liverpool) on [King's Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King's_Dock,_Port_of_Liverpool), [Alexandra Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandra_Tower,_Liverpool) and [1 Princes Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1_Princes_Dock) on [Prince's Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince's_Dock,_Liverpool) and [Liverpool Marina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Marina) around [Coburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coburg_Dock) and [Brunswick Docks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunswick_Dock). [The Wheel of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wheel_of_Liverpool) opened on 25 March 2010.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352]

However, plans to redevelop parts of [Liverpool city centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_city_centre) have been marred by [controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controversy). In December 2016, a newly formed company called [North Point Global Ltd.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Point_Global_Ltd.) was given the rights to develop part of the docks under the "New Chinatown" working name. Though heavily advertised in Liverpool, Hong Kong and Chinese cities with high-profile advertisements and videos, the "New Chinatown" development failed to materialise.[^cite_note-at-353] In January 2018, the *[Liverpool Echo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Echo)* and *[Asia Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Times)* revealed that the site remained sans any construction. North Point Global as well as its subcontractor "Bilt" had both declared bankruptcy, and the small investors (mostly middle class couples) who had already paid money for the apartments had lost most of their savings in them.[^cite_note-echo-354]

### Commercial district and cultural quarter



[St George's Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_George's_Hall,_Liverpool)



[Municipal Buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_Buildings,_Liverpool)

Liverpool's historic position as one of the most important trading ports in the world has meant that over time many grand buildings have been constructed in the city as headquarters for shipping firms, insurance companies, banks and other large firms. The great wealth this brought then allowed for the development of grand civic buildings, which were designed to allow the local administrators to 'run the city with pride'.[^cite_note-Liverpool_City_of_Architecture-355]

The [commercial district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_district) is centred on the [Castle Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Street,_Liverpool), [Dale Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Street) and Old Hall Street areas of the city, with many of the area's roads still following their [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/England_in_the_Middle_Ages) layout. Having developed predominantly over a period of three centuries, the area is regarded as one of the most important architectural locations in the city, as recognised by its inclusion in Liverpool's [former World Heritage site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Maritime_Mercantile_City).[^cite_note-356]

The oldest building in the area is the [Grade I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grade_I) [listed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Listed_building) [Liverpool Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Town_Hall), which is located at the top of Castle Street and dates from 1754. Often regarded as the city's finest piece of [Georgian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgian_architecture), the building is known as one of the most extravagantly decorated civic buildings anywhere in Britain.[^cite_note-Liverpool_City_Council_(2005),_p74-357][^cite_note-358] Also on Castle Street is the Grade I listed [Bank of England Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_England,_Liverpool), constructed between 1845 and 1848, as one of only three provincial branches of the [national bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_England).[^cite_note-Liverpool_City_Council_(2005),_p74-357] Among the other buildings in the area are the [Tower Buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower_Buildings,_Liverpool), [Albion House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albion_House,_Liverpool) (the former [White Star Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Star_Line) headquarters), the [Municipal Buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_Buildings,_Liverpool) and [Oriel Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriel_Chambers),[^cite_note-359] which is considered to be one of the earliest [Modernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernist) style buildings ever built.[^cite_note-360]

The area around [William Brown Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Brown_Street) is referred to as the city's 'Cultural Quarter', owing to the presence of numerous civic buildings, including the [William Brown Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Brown_Library), [Walker Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walker_Art_Gallery), [Picton Reading Rooms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picton_Reading_Room_and_Hornby_Library) and [World Museum Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Museum_Liverpool). The area is dominated by [neo-classical architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-classical_architecture), of which the most prominent, [St George's Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._George's_Hall,_Liverpool),[^cite_note-361] is widely regarded as the best example of a neo-classical building anywhere in Europe.[^cite_note-362] A Grade I listed building, it was constructed between 1840 and 1855 to serve a variety of civic functions in the city and its doors are inscribed with "[S.P.Q.L.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SPQR)" (Latin *senatus populusque Liverpudliensis*), meaning "the senate and people of Liverpool". William Brown Street is also home to numerous public monuments and sculptures, including [Wellington's Column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington's_Column) and the [Steble Fountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steble_Fountain). Many others are located around the area, particularly in [St John's Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_John's_Gardens,_Liverpool), which was specifically developed for this purpose.[^cite_note-363] The William Brown Street area has been likened to a modern recreation of the [Roman Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Forum).[^cite_note-364]

### Other notable landmarks



[Speke Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speke_Hall) [Tudor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudor_style_architecture) [manor house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manor_house) is one of Liverpool's oldest buildings.



[Liverpool Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cathedral), the largest cathedral in the UK



[Sefton Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sefton_Park) Palm House

While the majority of Liverpool's architecture dates from the mid-18th century onwards, there are several buildings that pre-date this time. One of the oldest surviving buildings is [Speke Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speke_Hall), a [Tudor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudor_style_architecture) [manor house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manor_house) located in the south of the city, which was completed in 1598.[^cite_note-365] The building is one of the few remaining [timber framed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timber_framed) Tudor houses left in the [north of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_of_England) and is particularly noted for its [Victorian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victorian_decorative_arts) interiors, which were added in the mid-19th century.[^cite_note-366] In addition to Speke Hall, many of the city's other oldest surviving buildings are also former manor houses including [Croxteth Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Croxteth_Hall) and [Woolton Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woolton_Hall), which were completed in 1702 and 1704 respectively.[^cite_note-367]

The oldest building within the city centre is the [Grade I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grade_I) [listed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Listed_building) [Bluecoat Chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluecoat_Chambers),[^cite_note-368] which was built between 1717 and 1718. Constructed in British [Queen Anne style architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Anne_style_architecture),[^cite_note-Liverpool_City_Council_2005_p97-369][^cite_note-370] the building was influenced in part by the work of [Christopher Wren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Wren)[^cite_note-371] and was originally the home of the [Bluecoat School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Blue_Coat_School) (who later moved to a larger site in [Wavertree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavertree) in the south of the city). Since 1908, it has acted as a centre for arts in Liverpool.[^cite_note-Liverpool_City_Council_2005_p97-369]

Liverpool is noted for having two cathedrals, each of which imposes over the landscape around it.[^cite_note-372] The [Anglican Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cathedral), which was constructed between 1904 and 1978, is the largest cathedral in Britain[^cite_note-373] and the [fifth largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_church_buildings_in_the_world). Designed and built in [Gothic style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_style), it is regarded as one of the greatest buildings to have been constructed during the 20th century[^cite_note-374] and was described by former British [Poet Laureate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poet_Laureate), [John Betjeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Betjeman), as "one of the great buildings of the world".[^cite_note-375] The Roman Catholic [Metropolitan Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Metropolitan_Cathedral) was constructed between 1962 and 1967 and is known as one of the first cathedrals to break the traditional longitudinal design.[^cite_note-376]

In the 21st century, many parts of Liverpool's city centre have undergone significant redevelopment and regeneration after years of decline. So far, the largest of these developments has been [Liverpool One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_One), which saw almost £1 billion invested in the redevelopment of 42 acres (17 hectares) of land, providing new retail, commercial, residential and leisure space.[^cite_note-377] Around the north of the city centre, several new skyscrapers have also been constructed including the [RIBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RIBA) award-winning [Unity Buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unity_Buildings) and [West Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Tower), which at 140m is [Liverpool's tallest building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_and_structures_in_Liverpool). Many redevelopment schemes are also in progress including Circus,[^cite_note-378] [King's Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King's_Dock,_Port_of_Liverpool),[^cite_note-379] [Paddington Village](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_Quarter,_Liverpool)[^cite_note-380] and [Liverpool Waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Waters).[^cite_note-381]

There are many other notable buildings in Liverpool, including the [art deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_deco) [former terminal building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crowne_Plaza_Liverpool_John_Lennon_Airport_Hotel) of [Speke Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_John_Lennon_Airport), the [University of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Liverpool)'s [Victoria Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Building,_University_of_Liverpool), (which provided the inspiration for the term *[Red Brick University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Brick_universities)*), and the [Adelphi Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britannia_Adelphi_Hotel), which was in the past considered to be one of the finest hotels anywhere in the world.[^cite_note-382]

### Parks and gardens

The [Register of Historic Parks and Gardens of Special Historic Interest in England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Register_of_Historic_Parks_and_Gardens_of_Special_Historic_Interest_in_England) describes [Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside)'s [Victorian Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Parks_and_commons_in_Liverpool) as collectively the "most important in the country".[^cite_note-383] Liverpool has ten listed parks and cemeteries, including two Grade I and five Grade II\*, more than any other English city apart from London.[^cite_note-384]

## Transport

Main article: [Transport in Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Liverpool)

Further information: [Liverpool city centre § Transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_city_centre#Transport)

Liverpool has an extensive transport infrastructure that connects the city with its metropolitan area, the rest of the United Kingdom, Europe and the world. Various modes of transport provide considerable connections by road, rail, air and sea. The local network of buses, trains and ferries is managed by [Merseytravel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseytravel), on behalf of the [Liverpool City Region Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region_Combined_Authority) and the [Metro Mayor of the Liverpool City Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Mayor_of_the_Liverpool_City_Region). The Mayor and Combined Authority have control of a [devolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom) transport budget and associated transport powers for this local system. The city's major port and international airport provide global links for both passengers and freight.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386]

### National and international travel

#### Roads



The Liverpool city centre entrance to the Queensway tunnel under the River Mersey

The city sits at the centre of a much larger metropolitan area. Its suburbs run contiguously into the neighbouring boroughs defined by the ONS as the [Liverpool Built-up Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Built-up_Area). The wider City Region and Merseyside in general is densely urbanised and divided by several motorways and major roads. The city is served by a network [motorways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_motorways_in_the_United_Kingdom). The three motorways in closest proximity are the [M58](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M58_motorway) to the north, and [M62](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M62_motorway) and [M57](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M57_motorway) to the east.

To the north, the M58 motorway runs 12 miles and provides links to the neighbouring counties of [Lancashire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancashire) and [Greater Manchester](79819.md), finally joining with the M6 west of Wigan.[^cite_note-387]

To the east, the M62 runs across the north of England, ultimately connecting Liverpool with [Hull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston-upon-Hull) on the opposite coast, and along the route with several large cities including [Manchester](20206.md), [Leeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leeds) and [Bradford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bradford). The M62 also provides a connection to both the [M6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M6_motorway) and [M1 motorways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_motorway), providing indirect links to more distant areas including [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham), London, [Nottingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottingham), [Preston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preston,_Lancashire) and [Sheffield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheffield).[^cite_note-388][^cite_note-389]

To the east, but running north–south, the M57 acts as a 10-mile ring road for the city itself and links the M62 and M58 motorways, as well as the [A580 East Lancashire Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A580_road) providing a link directly to the [A5300](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A5300) that links to the bridges that cross the Mersey to the south of the city.[^cite_note-390]

To the south, Liverpool is connected to [Widnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widnes) and [Warrington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrington) via the [A562](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A562_road) and [A5300](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A5300) and across the River Mersey to [Runcorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runcorn) in [Cheshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheshire), via the [Silver Jubilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver_Jubilee_Bridge) and [Mersey Gateway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mersey_Gateway) bridges. The [M56 motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M56_motorway) then provides routes into parts of the neighbouring counties, with connections to the Wirral and North Wales, and a direct route to [Manchester Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_Airport).[^cite_note-391]

The [Kingsway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingsway_Tunnel) and [Queensway Tunnels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queensway_Tunnel) connect Liverpool with the settlements on the [Wirral Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirral_Peninsula) across the Mersey to the west, including [Birkenhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkenhead) and [Wallasey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallasey). The [A41 road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A41_road) and [M53 motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M53_motorway), which both begin in Birkenhead, link to Cheshire and [Shropshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shropshire) and via the [A55](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A55_road), to [North Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Wales).[^cite_note-392]

#### Railway



Liverpool Lime Street, one of the busiest railway stations in the UK outside London

[^cite_note-393]

The national network, which is managed by [Network Rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_Rail), provides Liverpool with connections to major towns and cities across England. The city's primary main line station is [Liverpool Lime Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Lime_Street_railway_station), which is the terminus for several lines into the city.

The station is served by six [train operating companies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_operating_trains_in_the_United_Kingdom): [Avanti West Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avanti_West_Coast), [East Midlands Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Midlands_Railway), [Northern Trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Trains), [Transport for Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_for_Wales_Rail), [TransPennine Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TransPennine_Express) and [West Midlands Trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Midlands_Trains).[^cite_note-394] Between them, the station is connected with direct services to numerous destinations including [London Euston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Euston_railway_station) (in 2 hours 8 minutes with [Pendolino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Rail_Class_390) trains), [Birmingham New Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_New_Street_railway_station), [Cleethorpes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleethorpes_railway_station), [Glasgow Central](314616.md), [Leeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leeds_railway_station), [Manchester Piccadilly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_Piccadilly_railway_station), [Newcastle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newcastle_railway_station), [Norwich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwich_railway_station), [Nottingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nottingham_railway_station), [Preston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preston_railway_station), [Sheffield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheffield_railway_station) and [York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/York_railway_station).[^cite_note-395][^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398]

Opened in 1836, Lime Street is the world's oldest main line terminus station still in use.[^cite_note-399] In the south of the city, [Liverpool South Parkway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_South_Parkway) provides a connection to the city's airport.

#### Port



Liverpool Cruise Terminal

The [Port of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Liverpool) connects passengers and [freight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cargo) to Liverpool from all around the world. Passenger ferry services depart from the city across the [Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea) to [Belfast](5046.md) and the [Isle of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Man). Services are provided by several companies, including the [Isle of Man Steam Packet Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_of_Man_Steam_Packet_Company), [P\&O Ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P\&O_Ferries) and [Stena Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stena_Line).

The [Liverpool Cruise Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cruise_Terminal) handles over 200,000 passengers and crew annually and is located alongside the [Pier Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pier_Head) in the city centre. Berthing facilities for long-distance passenger cruises are provided and served by a large number of different [cruise lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cruise_line). Ports in [Australia](4689264.md), [France](5843419.md), [Faroe Islands](17238607.md), [Iceland](14531.md), [North America](21139.md), [Norway](21241.md), [Spain](26667.md) and the [Caribbean](18956035.md) are served by the facility.[^cite_note-400][^cite_note-401][^cite_note-402] The cruise lines that call at Liverpool cruise terminal include the following:

- [Ambassador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambassador_Cruise_Line)

  [^cite_note-403]

- Atlas Ocean Voyages

  [^cite_note-404]

- [Azamara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azamara)

  [^cite_note-405]

- [Carnival Cruise Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnival_Cruise_Line)

  [^cite_note-406]

- [Celebrity Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celebrity_Cruises)

  [^cite_note-407]

- [Cunard Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cunard_Line)

  [^cite_note-408]

- [Disney Cruise Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Cruise_Line)

  [^cite_note-409]

- [Fred Olsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred._Olsen_Cruise_Lines)

  [^cite_note-410]

- [Hapag-Lloyd cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hapag-Lloyd_cruises)

  [^cite_note-411]

- [Holland America Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holland_America_Line)

  [^cite_note-412]

- Noble Caledonia

  [^cite_note-413]

- [Norwegian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Cruise_Line)

  [^cite_note-414]

- [P\&O Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P\&O_Cruises)

  [^cite_note-415]

- [Princess Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princess_Cruises)

  [^cite_note-416]

- [Regent Seven Seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regent_Seven_Seas_Cruises)

  [^cite_note-417]

- [Royal Caribbean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Caribbean_International)

  [^cite_note-418]

- [Saga Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saga_Cruises)

  [^cite_note-419]

- [Silversea Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silversea_Cruises)

  [^cite_note-420]

- [Viking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viking_\(cruise_line\))

  [^cite_note-421]

As of 2022, the Port of Liverpool is the fourth busiest port in the UK by freight tonnage, handling 33 million tonnes of [freight cargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cargo).[^cite_note-port01-422] It is the main port in the country for [transatlantic trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transatlantic_crossing) and the largest port on the west coast of the UK. The [Royal Seaforth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seaforth_Dock) and [Liverpool2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool2) container terminals are the port's two main terminals and handle a wide variety of cargo including containers, [liquid and dry bulk cargoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulk_cargo) such as coal and grain, [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass) and [roll-on/roll-off](1457484.md) cargoes such as cars and trucks.[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424][^cite_note-425][^cite_note-426][^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428] [Leeds and Liverpool Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leeds_and_Liverpool_Canal) runs into Liverpool city centre via [Liverpool Canal Link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Canal_Link) at Pier Head since 2009.[^cite_note-429]

#### Airport



Liverpool John Lennon Airport terminal building

[Liverpool John Lennon Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_John_Lennon_Airport), which is located in the south of the city, provides direct air connections across the United Kingdom and Europe. It offers direct services to over 60 airports worldwide and to over 100 destinations via one-stop [connections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Layover) in [Frankfurt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankfurt_Airport), [Dublin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dublin_Airport) and [Keflavík](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keflav%C3%ADk_International_Airport). The airport is primarily served by [low-cost airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-cost_airlines) namely [Aer Lingus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aer_Lingus), [easyJet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EasyJet), [Jet2.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet2.com), [Loganair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loganair), [Lufthansa](97733.md), [Ryanair](262878.md), [Widerøe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wider%C3%B8e) and [Wizz Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wizz_Air), although it does provide facilities for [private aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_aviation).[^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431][^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434]

### Local travel

#### Railway



A [Class 777](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Rail_Class_777) train operated by Merseyrail

Liverpool's [urban railway network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commuter_rail_in_the_United_Kingdom), known as [Merseyrail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyrail), is one of the busiest and most extensive in the country. The network provides approximately 30 million passenger journeys per year, across a system of 69 stations throughout Liverpool's metropolitan area, within the formal boundaries of the Liverpool city region and adjacent areas of Cheshire and Lancashire.[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436][^cite_note-437][^cite_note-438]

The network consists of three lines:&#x20;

- the [Northern Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Line_\(Merseyrail\)), which runs to [Southport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southport_railway_station), [Ormskirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ormskirk_railway_station), [Headbolt Lane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Headbolt_Lane_railway_station) and [Hunts Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunts_Cross_railway_station)

- the [Wirral Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirral_Line), which runs through the Mersey Railway Tunnel and has branches to [New Brighton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Brighton_railway_station), [West Kirby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Kirby_railway_station), [Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester_railway_station) and [Ellesmere Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellesmere_Port_railway_station)

- the [City Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Line_\(Merseyrail\)), which begins at Lime Street, providing links to [St Helens Junction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Helens_Junction_railway_station), [Wigan North Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wigan_North_Western_railway_station), [Preston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preston_railway_station), [Warrington Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warrington_Central_railway_station) and [Manchester Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchester_Victoria_railway_station).

  [^cite_note-439]

  [^cite_note-440]

The network is predominantly electric and covers 75 miles (120 kilometres) of track.[^cite_note-Who_are_Merseyrail-441][^cite_note-442] Trains are owned and operated by the [Merseyrail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyrail) franchise and managed by [Merseytravel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseytravel) under the direction of the [Liverpool City Region Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region_Combined_Authority). Local services on the City Line are operated by [Northern Trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Trains), although the line itself remains part of the Merseyrail network. Within Liverpool city centre, four stations and over 6+1⁄2 miles (10.5 kilometres) of tunnels are underground.[^cite_note-Who_are_Merseyrail-441] [Birkenhead Hamilton Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkenhead_Hamilton_Square_railway_station) and [Liverpool James Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_James_Street_railway_station) are the oldest deep level underground stations in the world.[^cite_note-443]

In 2023, for the first time in UK history, [battery-powered passenger trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battery_electric_multiple_unit) launched on Merseyrail tracks from the newly opened Headbolt Lane station in Kirkby. The Liverpool City Region Combined Authority's long term *Merseyrail for All* plan is to reduce dependency on [live third rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_rail) and promote battery power in order to further expand Merseyrail to previously inaccessible places across the city region and as far as Manchester, Wrexham, Warrington and Preston.[^cite_note-444][^cite_note-445]

#### Buses



Liverpool South Parkway, a bus & rail interchange serving south Liverpool and Liverpool John Lennon Airport

Local bus services within and around Liverpool are managed by [Merseytravel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseytravel)[^cite_note-446] and are run by several different companies, including [Arriva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arriva_North_West) and [Stagecoach Merseyside and South Lancashire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stagecoach_Merseyside_and_South_Lancashire). The two principal termini for local buses are [Queen Square bus station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Square_bus_station) (located near Lime Street station) for services north and east of the city, and [Liverpool One bus station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_One_bus_station) (located near the [Royal Albert Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Dock,_Liverpool)) for services to the south and east.[^cite_note-447] [Cross-river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Mersey) services to the [Wirral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Wirral) use roadside terminus points in Castle Street and Sir Thomas Street. A [night bus service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_bus_service) also operates on Saturdays providing services from the city centre across Liverpool and wider region.[^cite_note-448]

[Tour bus services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_bus_service) are provided by Maghull Coaches which allow tourists to [hop-on-hop-off](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Sightseeing) and view historical landmarks and attractions, as well as [Liverpool F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_F.C.) and [Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles) related locations.[^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450] [National Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Express) services operate from the [Liverpool One bus station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_One_bus_station) to and from destinations across the UK.[^cite_note-451] In 2023, the Liverpool city region confirmed plans to become the second place outside London to implement [bus franchising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_franchising). Local leaders have argued that it will improve services by transferring control over fares, ticketing and routes from bus companies to the [Combined Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_Region_Combined_Authority). The full implementation of bus franchising will take place by the end of 2028.[^cite_note-452][^cite_note-453]

#### Mersey Ferry



A Mersey Ferry (foreground) with the Liverpool waterfront in the distance

The cross-river ferry service in Liverpool, known as the [Mersey Ferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mersey_Ferry), is managed and operated by [Merseytravel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseytravel), with services operating between the [Pier Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pier_Head) in Liverpool city centre and both [Woodside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodside,_Merseyside) in [Birkenhead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birkenhead) and [Seacombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seacombe) in [Wallasey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallasey). Services operate at intervals ranging from 20 minutes, at peak times, to every hour during the middle of the day and at weekends.[^cite_note-454] Despite remaining an important transport link between the city and the Wirral Peninsula, the Mersey Ferry has become an increasingly popular tourist attraction within the city, with daytime River Explorer Cruises providing passengers with an historical overview of the River Mersey and surrounding areas.[^cite_note-455]

### Cycling and scooters

A [scooter-sharing system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scooter-sharing_system) and [electric bicycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_bicycle) scheme operates throughout Liverpool which allows residents and visitors to move around the city on rented scooters and bicycles. The scheme is operated by Swedish technology company Voi; riders are able to pick up and drop off bikes and scooters at various locations around the city.[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457][^cite_note-458] [National Cycle Route 56](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cycle_Route_56), [National Cycle Route 62](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Cycle_Route_62) and National Cycle Route 810 run through Liverpool.

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Liverpool)

As with other large cities, Liverpool is an important cultural centre within the United Kingdom, incorporating music, performing arts, museums and art galleries, literature and nightlife among others. In 2008, the cultural heritage of the city was celebrated with the city holding the title of [European Capital of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Capital_of_Culture), during which time a wide range of cultural celebrations took place in the city, including [Go Superlambananas!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Go_Superlambananas!) and [La Princesse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Princesse).
