Liverpool has also held Europe's largest music and poetry event, the [Welsh national Eisteddfod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Eisteddfod_of_Wales), three times, despite being in England, in 1884, 1900, and 1929.

### Music

Main articles: [Music of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Liverpool) and [Beat music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beat_music)



[The Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles) statue at [Pier Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pier_Head). The group are the most commercially successful and critically acclaimed band in popular music.

[^cite_note-459]

Liverpool is internationally known for music and is recognised by *Guinness World Records* as the "World Capital City of Pop".[^cite_note-460] Musicians from the city have produced 58 No. 1 singles, more than any other city in the world.[^cite_note-461] Both the most successful male band and [girl group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spice_Girls) in global music history have contained Liverpudlian members. Liverpool is most famous as the birthplace of [the Beatles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Beatles) and during the 1960s was at the forefront of the [Beat Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beat_Music) movement, which would eventually lead to the [British Invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Invasion). Many notable musicians of the time originated in the city including [Billy J. Kramer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_J._Kramer), [Cilla Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cilla_Black), [Gerry and the Pacemakers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerry_and_the_Pacemakers) and [the Searchers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Searchers_\(band\)). The influence of musicians from Liverpool, coupled with other cultural exploits of the time, such as the [Liverpool poets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_poets), prompted American poet [Allen Ginsberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_Ginsberg) to proclaim that the city was "the centre of consciousness of the human universe".[^cite_note-462] Other musicians from Liverpool include [Billy Fury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billy_Fury), [A Flock of Seagulls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Flock_of_Seagulls), [Echo & the Bunnymen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echo_&_the_Bunnymen), [Frankie Goes to Hollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankie_Goes_to_Hollywood), [Frankie Vaughan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankie_Vaughan), [Anathema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anathema_\(band\)), [Ladytron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ladytron), [the Zutons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Zutons), [Cast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cast_\(British_band\)), [Atomic Kitten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_Kitten) and [Rebecca Ferguson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebecca_Ferguson_\(singer\)). [The La's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_La's) 1990 hit single "[There She Goes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/There_She_Goes_\(The_La's_song\))" was described by *[Rolling Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolling_Stone)* as a "founding piece of [Britpop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Britpop)'s foundation."[^cite_note-463]



[Philharmonic Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philharmonic_Hall,_Liverpool), home of the [Royal Liverpool Philharmonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Liverpool_Philharmonic)

The city is also home to the oldest surviving professional [symphony orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symphony_orchestra) in the UK, the [Royal Liverpool Philharmonic Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Liverpool_Philharmonic_Orchestra), which is based in the [Philharmonic Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philharmonic_Hall,_Liverpool).[^cite_note-464] The chief conductor of the orchestra is [Vasily Petrenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasily_Petrenko).[^cite_note-465] Sir [Edward Elgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Elgar) dedicated his [Pomp and Circumstance March No. 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomp_and_Circumstance#March_No._1_in_D) to the Liverpool Orchestral Society, and the piece had its first performance in the city in 1901.[^cite_note-466] Among Liverpool's curiosities, the Austrian émigré [Fritz Spiegl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_Spiegl) is notable. He not only became a world expert on the etymology of [Scouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scouse), but composed the music to [Z-cars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z-cars) and the [Radio 4 UK Theme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_4_UK_Theme).

Well established festivals in the city include [Africa Oyé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa_Oy%C3%A9) and [Brazilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilica_Festival) which are the UK's largest free African and Brazilian music festivals respectively.[^cite_note-467][^cite_note-468] The dance music festival [Creamfields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creamfields) was established by the Liverpool-based [Cream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cream_\(nightclub\)) clubbing brand which started life as a weekly event at Nation nightclub. There are numerous music venues located across the city, however, the [Liverpool Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Arena) is by far the largest. Opened in 2008, the 11,000-seat arena hosted the [MTV Europe Music Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MTV_Europe_Music_Awards_2008) the same year, and since then has played host to world-renowned acts such as [Andrea Bocelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrea_Bocelli), [Beyoncé](83688.md), [Elton John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elton_John), [Kanye West](523032.md), [Kasabian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kasabian), [the Killers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Killers), [Lady Gaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_Gaga), [Oasis](161515.md), [Pink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pink_\(singer\)), [Rihanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rihanna), and [UB40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UB40).

On 7 October 2022, the [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Broadcasting_Corporation) and the [European Broadcasting Union](10380.md) (EBU) announced that Liverpool would host the [Eurovision Song Contest 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_2023) on behalf of the previous year's winning country [Ukraine](31750.md), which was unable to meet the demands of hosting the event due to security concerns caused by the [Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md). The contest was held at Liverpool Arena, and consisted of two semi-finals on 9 and 11 May and a final on 13 May 2023. This was the first time that the contest took place in the city, and was also a record-extending ninth time that the UK has hosted the contest, having last done so in [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham) in [1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest_1998).[^cite_note-469]

### Visual arts



[William Brown Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Brown_Street), also known as the Cultural Quarter, was a [World Heritage Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Maritime_Mercantile_City) consisting of the [World Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Museum), [Central Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Central_Library), [Picton Reading Room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picton_Reading_Room_and_Hornby_Library) and [Walker Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walker_Art_Gallery).

Liverpool has more galleries and national museums than any other city in the United Kingdom apart from London.[^cite_note-visitliverpool-13] [National Museums Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museums_Liverpool) is the only English national collection based wholly outside London.[^cite_note-470] The [Tate Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tate_Liverpool) gallery houses the modern art collection of the Tate in the North of England and was, until the opening of [Tate Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tate_Modern), the largest exhibition space dedicated to modern art in the United Kingdom. The [FACT centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FACT_centre) hosts touring multimedia exhibitions, while the [Walker Art Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walker_Art_Gallery) houses one of the most impressive permanent collections of [Pre-Raphaelite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Raphaelite) art in the world.[^cite_note-471] [Sudley House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudley_House) contains another major collection of pre-20th-century art.[^cite_note-liverpoolmuseums-472] Liverpool University's [Victoria Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Gallery_&_Museum) was re-opened as a public art gallery and museum to display the university's artwork and historical collections which include the largest display of art by [Audubon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_James_Audubon) outside the US.[^cite_note-473] A number of artists have also come from the city, including painter [George Stubbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Stubbs) who was born in Liverpool in 1724.



[Nelson Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nelson_Monument,_Liverpool) at [Exchange Flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exchange_Flags). A short distance away another noted commander from the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars) is commemorated by [Wellington's Column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wellington's_Column).

The [Liverpool Biennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Biennial) festival of arts runs from mid-September to late November and comprises three main sections; the International, The Independents and New Contemporaries although fringe events are timed to coincide.[^cite_note-biennial-474] It was during the 2004 festival that [Yoko Ono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoko_Ono)'s work "My mummy was beautiful" invited controversy when photographs of a woman's breast and crotch were exhibited on the main shopping street.[^cite_note-475]

### Literature

[Felicia Hemans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felicia_Hemans) (née Browne) was born in Dale Street, Liverpool, in 1793, although she later moved to Flintshire, in Wales. Felicia was born in Liverpool, a granddaughter of the [Venetian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Venice) [consul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consulate_general) in that city. Her father's business soon brought the family to [Denbighshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denbighshire_\(historic\)) in North Wales, where she spent her youth. They made their home near Abergele and [St. Asaph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Asaph) ([Flintshire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flintshire_\(historic\))), and it is clear that she came to regard herself as Welsh by adoption, later referring to Wales as "Land of my childhood, my home and my dead". Her first poems, dedicated to the [Prince of Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_IV_of_the_United_Kingdom), were published in Liverpool in 1808, when she was only fourteen, arousing the interest of [Percy Bysshe Shelley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Percy_Bysshe_Shelley), who briefly corresponded with her. [^cite_note-inspidered.wordpress.com-476]

An engraving of a painting of  [The Wishing Gate](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Letitia%20Elizabeth%20Landon%20\(L.%20E.%20L.\)%20in%20Fisher's%20Drawing%20Room%20Scrap%20Book,%201834/The%20Wishing%20Gate). by S. F. Serres was published in Fisher's Drawing Room Scrap Book, 1834 with a poetical illustration by [Letitia Elizabeth Landon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letitia_Elizabeth_Landon) to which she adds the note 'I believe that to this haunted gate, a common superstition is attached, namely, that to wish, and to have that wish fulfilled, is the result of such wish being uttered while passing'. It stood on the North Shore before the docks were built and was a place where farewells could be waved to departing voyagers.[^cite_note-477]



Plaque in Campbell Square commemorating [Charles Dickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Dickens) who from 1842 gave public readings of his novels at [St George's Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_George's_Hall,_Liverpool), and who for one day in 1860 was appointed a special [police constable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Constabulary) in the city while researching a novel.

A number of notable authors have visited Liverpool, including [Daniel Defoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Defoe), [Washington Irving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Irving), [Charles Dickens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Dickens), [Thomas De Quincey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_De_Quincey), [Herman Melville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herman_Melville), [Nathaniel Hawthorne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathaniel_Hawthorne), [Gerard Manley Hopkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerard_Manley_Hopkins) and [Hugh Walpole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Walpole). Defoe, after visiting the city, described it, as "one of the wonders of Britain in his 'Tour through England and Wales'".[^cite_note-478] Melville's novel *[Redburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redburn)* deals with the first seagoing voyage of 19 years old Wellingborough Redburn between New York and Liverpool in 1839. Largely autobiographical, the middle sections of the book are set in Liverpool and describe the young merchantman's wanderings, and his reflections.[^cite_note-inspidered.wordpress.com-476] From 1842 to 1869, Dickens visited the city to give public readings of his novels.[^cite_note-479] Hawthorne was stationed in Liverpool as United States consul between 1853 and 1856.[^cite_note-480] Hopkins served as priest at St Francis Xavier Church, Langdale St., Liverpool, between 1879 and 81.[^cite_note-481] Although he is not known to have ever visited Liverpool, [Jung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jung) famously had a vivid dream of the city which he analysed in one of his works.[^cite_note-482]

*[Her Benny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Her_Benny_\(novel\))*, a novel telling the tragic story of Liverpool street urchins in the 1870s, written by Methodist preacher [Silas K. Hocking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silas_K._Hocking), was a best-seller and the first book to sell a million copies in the author's lifetime.[^cite_note-483] The prolific writer of adventure novels, [Harold Edward Bindloss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Edward_Bindloss) (1866–1945), was born in Liverpool.



[Clive Barker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clive_Barker), Liverpool born writer of *[Hellraiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellraiser)* and creator of [Candyman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candyman_\(character\))

The writer, docker and political activist [George Garrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Garrett_\(activist\)) was born in [Seacombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seacombe), on the [Wirral Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirral_Peninsula) in 1896 and was brought up in Liverpool's South end, around Park Road, the son of a fierce Liverpool–Irish Catholic mother and a staunch 'Orange' stevedore father. In the 1920s and 1930s, his organisation within the Seamen's Vigilance Committees, unemployed demonstrations, and hunger marches from Liverpool became part of a wider cultural force. He spoke at reconciliation meetings in sectarian Liverpool, and helped found the [Unity Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unity_Theatre,_Liverpool) in the 1930s as part of the [Popular Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front) against the rise of fascism, particularly its echoes in the [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War). Garrett died in 1966.[^cite_note-484]

The novelist and playwright [James Hanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Hanley_\(novelist\)) (1897–1985) was born in [Kirkdale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirkdale,_Merseyside), Liverpool, in 1897 (not [Dublin](8504.md), nor 1901 as he generally implied) to a working-class family.[^cite_note-485] Hanley grew up close to the docks and much of his early writing is about seamen. *[The Furys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Furys_Chronicle)* (1935) is first in a sequence of five loosely autobiographical novels about working-class life in Liverpool. James Hanley's brother, novelist [Gerald Hanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Hanley) (1916–92) was also born in Liverpool (not [County Cork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_Cork), Ireland, as he claimed).[^cite_note-486] While he published a number of novels he also wrote [radio plays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_drama) for the BBC as well as some film scripts, most notably *[The Blue Max](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Blue_Max)* (1966).[^cite_note-487] He was also one of several scriptwriters for a life of [Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandhi) (1964).[^cite_note-488] Novelist [Beryl Bainbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beryl_Bainbridge) (1932–2010) was born in Liverpool and raised in nearby [Formby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formby). She was primarily known for her works of [psychological fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_fiction), often set among the English working classes. Bainbridge won the [Whitbread Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa_Book_Awards) prize for best novel in [1977](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Whitbread_Awards) and [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Whitbread_Awards) and was nominated five times for the [Booker Prize](4446.md). *[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)* newspaper named Bainbridge among their list of "The 50 greatest [British writers](296550.md) since 1945".[^cite_note-489]

[J. G. Farrell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._G._Farrell) was born in Liverpool in 1935 but left at the outbreak of war in 1939.[^cite_note-490] A novelist of Irish descent, Farrell gained prominence for his historical fiction, most notably his *Empire Trilogy* (*[Troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troubles_\(novel\))*, *[The Siege of Krishnapur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Siege_of_Krishnapur)* and *[The Singapore Grip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Singapore_Grip)*), dealing with the political and human consequences of British colonial rule. However, his career ended when he drowned in Ireland in 1979 at the age of 44.

[Helen Forrester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Forrester) was the pen name of June Bhatia (née Huband) (1919–2011),[^cite_note-Obituary-491][^cite_note-492] who was known for her books about her early childhood in Liverpool during the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), including *Twopence to Cross the Mersey* (1974), as well as several works of fiction. During the late 1960s the city became well known for the [Liverpool poets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_poets), who include [Roger McGough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_McGough) and the late [Adrian Henri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrian_Henri). An anthology of poems, *[The Mersey Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mersey_Sound_\(book\))*, written by Henri, McGough and [Brian Patten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Patten), has sold well since it was first being published in 1967.

Liverpool has produced several noted writers of horror fiction, often set on Merseyside—[Ramsey Campbell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramsey_Campbell), [Clive Barker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clive_Barker) and Peter Atkins among them. A collection of Liverpudlian horror fiction, *Spook City* was edited by a Liverpool expatriate, Angus Mackenzie, and introduced by Doug Bradley, also from Liverpool.[^cite_note-493] Bradley is famed for portraying Barker's creation Pinhead in the *Hellraiser* series of films.

### Performing arts



The [Empire Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Empire_Theatre) has the largest two-tier auditorium in the UK.

Liverpool also has a long history of performing arts, reflected in several annual theatre festivals such as the [Liverpool Shakespeare Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Shakespeare_Festival), which takes place inside [Liverpool Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cathedral) and in the adjacent historic St James' Gardens every summer; the Everyword Festival of new theatre writing, the only one of its kind in the country;[^cite_note-494] Physical Fest, an international festival of physical theatre;[^cite_note-495] the annual festivals organised by [Liverpool John Moores University](395962.md)'s drama department and the [Liverpool Institute for Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Institute_for_Performing_Arts); and other festivals by the large number of theatres in the city, such as the [Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Empire_Theatre), [Everyman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everyman_Theatre,_Liverpool),[^cite_note-496][^cite_note-497] [Playhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Playhouse),[^cite_note-everymanplayhouse-498][^cite_note-unitytheatre-499] [Royal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Court_Theatre,_Liverpool), and [Unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unity_Theatre,_Liverpool) theatres.

Notable actors and actresses from Liverpool include [Arthur Askey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Askey), [Tom Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Baker), [Kim Cattrall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Cattrall), [Jodie Comer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jodie_Comer), [Stephen Graham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Graham), [Rex Harrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rex_Harrison), [Jason Isaacs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Isaacs), [Tina Malone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tina_Malone), the McGann brothers ([Joe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_McGann), [Mark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_McGann), [Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_McGann), and [Stephen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_McGann)), [David Morrissey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Morrissey), [Elizabeth Morton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Morton), [Peter Serafinowicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Serafinowicz), [Elisabeth Sladen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elisabeth_Sladen), [Alison Steadman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alison_Steadman), and [Rita Tushingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rita_Tushingham). Actors and actresses from elsewhere in the world have strong ties to the city, such as Canadian actor [Mike Myers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Myers) (whose parents were both from Liverpool) and American actress [Halle Berry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halle_Berry) (whose mother was from Liverpool).

### Nightlife



Nightlife in [Mathew Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathew_Street) and Temple Court, [Liverpool city centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_city_centre)

Liverpool has a thriving and varied nightlife. The majority of the city's late-night restaurants, bars, pubs, nightclubs, music venues and comedy clubs are located in a number of distinct districts.

In 2023, figures from global data company [Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square_\(financial_services\)) show that night-time spending in bars and restaurants in [Liverpool city centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_city_centre) outperformed all [major UK cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_status_in_the_United_Kingdom), including London.[^cite_note-500]

Figures by the Liverpool BID Company suggest that the busiest nights of the week in [Liverpool city centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_city_centre) are Friday and Saturday. Using cameras to track the flow of people in key locations between 7 pm and 4 am, at least 1.5 million people pass through the city centre every Friday night and almost 2 million people on Saturday nights. The data demonstrates that Monday night is the quietest night of the week in the city centre and footfall then increases every single night to reach its peak on Saturday nights. 125,889 people worked in the city's night time economy as of 2022, according to the Liverpool BID Company.[^cite_note-501][^cite_note-502][^cite_note-503]

Liverpool's nightlife is concentrated in a number of districts including [Ropewalks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RopeWalks,_Liverpool) which comprises [Concert Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concert_Square,_Liverpool), St. Peter's Square and the adjoining Seel Street and [Duke Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Street,_Liverpool). Other popular areas include [Hardman Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hardman_Street), the [Cavern Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathew_Street), [Baltic Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Triangle), [Royal Albert Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Dock,_Liverpool) and the city's [Pride Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pride_Quarter,_Liverpool), which is home to a large number of [LGBT venues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_bar).[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505]

In the city's suburbs, [Lark Lane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lark_Lane,_Liverpool) in [Aigburth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aigburth) is noted for an abundance of bars and late-night venues.[^cite_note-506][^cite_note-507]

## Education

See also: [List of schools in Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_schools_in_Liverpool)



[University of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Liverpool)'s [Victoria Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Building,_University_of_Liverpool)

In Liverpool primary and secondary education is available in various forms supported by the state including secular, [Church of England](5955.md), Jewish, and Roman Catholic. [Islamic](6037917.md) education is available at primary level, but there is no secondary provision. One of Liverpool's important early schools was [The Liverpool Blue Coat School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Blue_Coat_School); founded in 1708 as a charitable school. It is now a [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_school) [grammar school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammar_school).

The Liverpool Blue Coat School is the top-performing school in the city with 100% 5 or more A\*-C grades at GCSE resulting in the 30th best GCSE results in the country and an average point score per student of 1087.4 in A/AS levels.[^cite_note-508] Other notable schools include [Liverpool College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_College) founded in 1840 [Merchant Taylors' School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merchant_Taylors'_School,_Northwood) founded in 1620.[^cite_note-liverpoolcollege-509] Another of Liverpool's notable senior schools is [St. Edward's College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Edward's_College) situated in the West Derby area of the city. Historic grammar schools, such as the [Liverpool Institute High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Institute_High_School_for_Boys) and [Liverpool Collegiate School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Collegiate_School)—both closed in the 1980s—are still remembered as centres of academic excellence. Bellerive Catholic College is the city's top-performing non-selective school, based upon GCSE results in 2007.



[Liverpool John Moores University](395962.md)'s [James Parsons Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Parsons_Building)

Liverpool has three universities: the [University of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Liverpool), [Liverpool John Moores University](395962.md) and [Liverpool Hope University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Hope_University). [Edge Hill University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edge_Hill_University), founded as a [teacher-training college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_school) in the [Edge Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edge_Hill,_Liverpool) district of Liverpool, is now located in [Ormskirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ormskirk) in South-West Lancashire. Liverpool is also home to the [Liverpool Institute for Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Institute_for_Performing_Arts) (LIPA).

The [University of Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Liverpool) was established in 1881 as University College Liverpool. In 1884, it became part of the federal [Victoria University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_University_\(United_Kingdom\)). Following a Royal Charter and Act of Parliament in 1903, it became an independent university, the University of Liverpool, with the right to confer its own degrees. It was the first university to offer degrees in biochemistry, architecture, civic design, veterinary science, oceanography and social science.



[City of Liverpool College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Liverpool_College)'s Arts Centre

[Liverpool Hope University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Hope_University), which was formed through the merger of three colleges, the earliest of which was founded in 1844, gained university status in 2005. It is the only ecumenical university in Europe.[^cite_note-510] It is situated on both sides of Taggart Avenue in Childwall and has a second campus in the city centre (the Cornerstone).

The [Liverpool School of Tropical Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_School_of_Tropical_Medicine), founded to address some of the problems created by trade, continues today as a post-graduate school affiliated with the University of Liverpool and houses an anti-venom repository.

[Liverpool John Moores University](395962.md) was previously a [polytechnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_technology), and gained status in 1992. It is named in honour of [Sir John Moores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Moores_\(British_businessman\)), one of the founders of the [Littlewoods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Very_Group) football pools and retail group, who was a major benefactor. The institution was previously owned and run by Liverpool City Council. It traces it lineage to the Liverpool [Mechanics' institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanics'_institute), opened in 1823, making it by this measure England's third-oldest university.

The city has one [further education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Further_education) college, [City of Liverpool College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Liverpool_College) in the city centre. Liverpool City Council operates [Burton Manor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burton_Manor), a residential [adult education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adult_education) college in nearby [Burton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burton_\(near_Neston\)), on the [Wirral Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wirral_Peninsula).

There are two Jewish schools in Liverpool, both belonging to the King David Foundation. [King David School, Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_David_High_School,_Liverpool), is the High School and the King David Primary School. There is also a King David Kindergarten, featured in the community centre of Harold House. These schools are all run by the King David Foundation located in Harold House in Childwall; conveniently next door to the [Childwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childwall) Synagogue.

## Sport

### Football



The [Merseyside derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside_derby) is the football match between the two biggest clubs in the city; [Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_F.C.) in red and [Everton](91155.md) in blue

Liverpool is one of the most successful footballing cities in England, and is home to two top flight [Premier League](11250.md) teams. [Everton F.C.](91155.md) was founded in 1878 and was one of the twelve founder members of the [Football League](14419588.md). It plays at [Hill Dickinson Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_Dickinson_Stadium). [Liverpool F.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_F.C.) were founded in 1892 and play at [Anfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfield). Between them, the clubs have won 28 English First Division titles, 12 [FA Cup](11237.md) titles, 10 [League Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EFL_Cup) titles, 6 [European Cup](44220.md) titles, 1 [FIFA Club World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_Club_World_Cup) title, 1 [European Cup Winners' Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Cup_Winners'_Cup) title, 3 [UEFA Cup](232175.md) titles, and 24 [FA Charity Shields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FA_Community_Shield).

The two clubs contest the [Merseyside derby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside_derby), dubbed the 'friendly derby'. Despite the name the fixture is known for its keen rivalry, having seen more sending-offs in this fixture than any other. Unlike many other derbies it is not rare for families in the city to contain supporters of both clubs.[^cite_note-511] Liverpool F.C. is the English and British club with the most European Cup titles with six, the latest in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_UEFA_Champions_League_final).



[Anfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfield), home of Liverpool F.C.

[Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_F.C.) has played at [Anfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfield) since 1892, when the club was formed to occupy the stadium following [Everton's](91155.md) departure due to a dispute with their landlord. Liverpool are still playing there 125 years later, although the ground has been completely rebuilt since the 1970s. The Spion Kop (rebuilt as an all-seater stand in 1994–95) was the most famous part of the ground, gaining cult status across the world due to the songs and celebrations of the many fans who packed onto its terraces. Anfield as capacity for 54,000 spectators in comfort and is a distinctive landmark in an area filled with smaller and older buildings. Liverpool club also has a multimillion-pound youth training facility called The Academy.



[Goodison Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodison_Park), home of Everton W\.F.C.

&#x20;After leaving Anfield in 1892, [Everton](91155.md) moved to [Goodison Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodison_Park) on the opposite side of [Stanley Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Park,_Liverpool). The ground was opened on 24 August 1892, by Lord Kinnaird and Frederick Wall of the FA but the first crowds to attend the ground saw a short athletics meeting followed by a selection of music and a fireworks display. Everton's first game there was on 2 September 1892 when they beat Bolton 4–2. It was one of the host venues during the [1966 FIFA World Cup](61629.md). It now has the capacity for just under 40,000 spectators all-seated, but the last expansion took place in 1994 when a new Park End Stand gave the stadium an all-seater capacity. The Goodison Road Stand dates back to the 1970s, while the Gwladys Street Stand and Bullens Road Stand are refurbished pre-Second World War structures.

[Everton](91155.md) has recently completed the process of relocating, with a stadium move first mooted as early as 1996.[^cite_note-512] In 2003, the club were forced to abandon plans for a 55,000-seat stadium at [King's Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King's_Dock,_Port_of_Liverpool) due to financial constraints,[^cite_note-513] with further proposed moves to Kirkby (comprising part of [Destination Kirkby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Kirkby_Project), moving the stadium just beyond Liverpool's council boundary into [Kirkby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirkby)) and Walton Hall Park similarly scrapped.

The club relocated to the multimillion-pound [Everton Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everton_Stadium) designed by the American architect [Dan Meis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Meis) at the nearby [Bramley-Moore Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bramley-Moore_Dock) on the [River Mersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Mersey) waterfront for the beginning of the 2025/26 season, with ground broken on the project in August 2021.[^cite_note-514] The new stadium will have a capacity of 52,888 which could be expanded to 62,000 demand permitting and it will be a host venue for the [UEFA Euro 2028](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2028). Everton also have a multimillion-pound training facility based at [Finch Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finch_Farm). The [Everton Women's Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everton_F.C._\(women\)) play in the [Women's Super League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_Super_League) and moved into Goodison Park following the men's team's exit, having previously played at [Walton Hall Park Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walton_Hall_Park_\(stadium\)).

### Rugby league

Despite being a popular sport in Northern England, professional rugby league failed to develop in the Liverpool city region. [Liverpool City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_City_\(1906\)) was the city's first professional club, but only competed in the [1906–07 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1906%E2%80%9307_Northern_Rugby_Football_Union_season) where they finished 26th of 26. Despite the failure to establish a team, the [Northern Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_Football_League) scheduled four [Kangaroo Tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangaroo_Tour) matches at [Goodison Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodison_Park) in their first three tours to Great Britain between 1908 and 1922, hosting three English League XIII games and one [Lancashire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancashire_rugby_league_team) game.[^cite_note-\:0-515]

Professional rugby league did not return to the city until 1934 with the formation of [Liverpool Stanley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Stanley). The following year, [England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/England_national_rugby_league_team) played [Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wales_national_rugby_league_team) at the club's ground in the [1935 European Rugby League Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1935_European_Rugby_League_Championship). Stanley competed until 1968 twice reaching the playoffs. The club hosted [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_national_rugby_league_team) in their [1956–57 tour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1956%E2%80%9357_Kangaroo_tour_of_Great_Britain_and_France), the first time the Kangaroos had played in Liverpool since 1922.

Professional rugby league was again absent from the city until [Anfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfield) hosted the 1989 [Charity Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_League_Charity_Shield_\(Great_Britain\)) where [Widnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widnes_Vikings) beat [Wigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wigan_Warriors) 27–22. Two years later, the stadium hosted the [1991 World Club Challenge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_World_Club_Challenge) where Wigan beat [Penrith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penrith_Panthers) 21–4. Trying to capitalise on the growing interest in the sport, not just in Liverpool but across the country, [St Helens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Helens_R.F.C.) (the only professional club in the wider [Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merseyside) region) held a number of home games at Anfield in the mid to late 1990s. However wider problems with the sport as a result of the [Super League war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_League_war) stopped this initiative.

The professional game would again be absent from city until the mid and late 2010s when [Anfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anfield) again hosted the [2016 Rugby League Four Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Rugby_League_Four_Nations) final, Test 2 of the [2018 Baskerville Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_New_Zealand_rugby_league_tour_of_England#Baskerville_Shield), and [Magic Weekend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_Weekend) in 2019. 40,042 attended the Four Nations final.[^cite_note-\:0-515]

Everton's new [Bramley-Moore Dock Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bramley-Moore_Dock_Stadium) will host Test 2 of the [2025 Ashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Kangaroo_tour_of_England_and_France).[^cite_note-\:0-515]

### Boxing

Main article: [Boxing in Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boxing_in_Liverpool)

Boxing is massively popular in Liverpool. The city has a proud heritage and history in the sport and is home to around 22 amateur boxing clubs, which are responsible for producing many successful boxers, such as [Nel Tarleton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nel_Tarleton), [Alan Rudkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Rudkin), [John Conteh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Conteh), [Andy Holligan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Holligan), [Liam Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liam_Smith_\(boxer\)), [Paul Hodkinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Hodkinson), [Tony Bellew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Bellew) and [Robin Ried](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Reid_\(boxer\)). The city also boasts a consistently strong amateur contingent which is highlighted by Liverpool being the most represented city on the [GB Boxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GB_Boxing) team, as well as at the [2012 London Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics), the most notable Liverpool amateur fighters include; [Jimmy Lloyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Lloyd_\(boxer\)), [George Turpin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Turpin), [Tony Willis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Willis), [Robin Reid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Reid_\(boxer\)) and [David Price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Price_\(boxer\)) who have all medalled at the [Olympic Games](22576.md). Boxing events are usually hosted at the [Echo Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Arena) and [Liverpool Olympia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Olympia) within the city, although the former home of Liverpool boxing was the renowned [Liverpool Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Stadium).

### Horse racing



The Earl of Derby Stand at [Aintree Racecourse](933419.md); home of the [Grand National](74730.md)

[Aintree Racecourse](933419.md) in the adjacent [Metropolitan Borough of Sefton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Sefton) is home to the world's most famous steeple-chase, the [Randox Grand National](74730.md) which takes place annually in early April. The race meeting attracts horse owners/ jockeys from around the world to compete in the demanding 4-mile (6.5-kilometre) and 30-fence course. There have been many memorable moments of the Grand National, for instance, the 100/1 outsider Foinavon in 1967, the dominant [Red Rum](2545868.md) and [Ginger McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginger_McCain) of the 1970s and [Mon Mome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mon_Mome) (100/1) who won the 2009 meeting. In 2010, the National became the first horse race to be televised in [high-definition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-definition_television) in the UK.

### Golf

&#x20;The [Royal Liverpool Golf Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Liverpool_Golf_Club), situated in the nearby town of [Hoylake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoylake) on the Wirral Peninsula, has hosted [The Open Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Open_Championship) on a number of occasions, most recently in [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Open_Championship). It also hosted the [Walker Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walker_Cup) in 1983.&#x20;



The [Royal Liverpool Golf Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Liverpool_Golf_Club), [Hoylake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoylake)

### Greyhound racing

Liverpool once contained four greyhound tracks, [Seaforth Greyhound Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seaforth_Greyhound_Stadium) (1933–1965), [Breck Park Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breck_Park_Stadium) (1927–1948), [Stanley Greyhound Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Greyhound_Stadium_\(Liverpool\)) (1927–1961) and [White City Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_City_Stadium_\(Liverpool\)) (1932–1973). Breck Park also hosted boxing bouts and both Stanley and Seaforth hosted [Motorcycle speedway](230182.md).

### Athletics

[Wavertree Sports Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavertree_Sports_Park) is home to the Liverpool Harriers athletics club, which has produced such athletes as [Curtis Robb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtis_Robb), [Allyn Condon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allyn_Condon) (the only British athlete to compete at both the Summer and Winter Olympics), and [Katarina Johnson-Thompson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katarina_Johnson-Thompson); Great Britain was represented by Johnson-Thompson at the [2012 London Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics) in the women's heptathlon, and she would go on to win the gold medal at the [2019 World Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_World_Athletics_Championships), giving Liverpool its first gold medal and breaking the [British record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_British_records_in_athletics) in the process.

### Gymnastics

In August 2012, Liverpool gymnast [Beth Tweddle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beth_Tweddle) won an Olympic bronze medal in [London 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics) in the uneven bars at her third [Olympic Games](22576.md), thus becoming the most decorated British gymnast in history. Park Road Gymnastics Centre provides training to a high level.

### Swimming

Liverpool has produced several swimmers who have represented their nation at major championships such as the [Olympic Games](22576.md). The most notable of which is [Steve Parry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Parry_\(swimmer\)) who claimed a bronze medal at the [2004 Athens Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Summer_Olympics) in the 200m butterfly. Others include Herbert Nickel Haresnape, [Margaret Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Kelly_\(swimmer\)), Shellagh Ratcliffe and Austin Rawlinson. There is a purpose-built aquatics centre at [Wavertree Sports Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavertree_Sports_Park), which opened in 2008. The City of Liverpool Swimming Club has been National Speedo League Champions 8 out of the last 11 years.

### Cricket



[Liverpool Cricket Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cricket_Club)

The city is the hub of the [Liverpool and District Cricket Competition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_and_District_Cricket_Competition), an [ECB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/England_and_Wales_Cricket_Board) [Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ECB_Premier_Leagues).[^cite_note-516] [Sefton Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sefton_Park_Cricket_Club) and [Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cricket_Club) are the league's founder members based in the city with Wavertree, Alder and Old Xaverians clubs having joined the league more recently.[^cite_note-517] Liverpool plays host [Lancashire County Cricket Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancashire_County_Cricket_Club) as an outground most seasons, including six of eight home [County Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_Championship) games during Lancashire's 2011[^cite_note-518] title winning campaign[^cite_note-519] while [Old Trafford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Trafford_Cricket_Ground) was refurbished.[^cite_note-520][^cite_note-Let_There_Be_Lights-521]

### Tennis

Since 2014 [Liverpool Cricket Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Cricket_Club) has played host[^cite_note-522] to the annual [Tradition-ICAP Liverpool International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_International_Tennis_Tournament) tennis tournament, which has seen tennis stars such as [Novak Djokovic](16100029.md), [David Ferrer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Ferrer), [Mardy Fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mardy_Fish), [Laura Robson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Robson) and [Caroline Wozniacki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Wozniacki). Previously this had been held at [Calderstones Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calderstones_Park), situated in [Allerton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allerton,_Liverpool) in the south of the city. Liverpool Tennis Development Programme at Wavertree Tennis Centre is one of the largest in the UK.

### Basketball



[Liverpool Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Arena) hosts numerous sporting events and was formerly the home of [British Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Basketball_League) team, the [Mersey Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mersey_Tigers).

Professional basketball came to the city in 2007 with the entry of Everton Tigers, later known as [Mersey Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mersey_Tigers), into the elite [British Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Basketball_League). The club was originally associated with Everton F.C., and was part of the *Toxteth Tigers* youth development programme, which reached over 1,500 young people every year.[^cite_note-Tigers-523] The Tigers began to play in Britain's top league for the [2007–08 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_British_Basketball_League_season), playing at the [Greenbank Sports Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenbank_Sports_Academy) before moving into the newly completed [Echo Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Arena) during that season. After the 2009–10 season, Everton F.C. withdrew funding from the Tigers, who then changed their name to Mersey Tigers. The club were expelled from the British Basketball League in 2013 due to financial problems.[^cite_note-524]

### Baseball

Liverpool is one of three cities which still host the traditional sport of [British baseball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_baseball) and it hosts the annual England-Wales international match every two years, alternating with [Cardiff](5882.md) and [Newport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newport,_Wales). Liverpool Trojans are the oldest existing baseball club in the UK.

### Cycling

The [2014 Tour of Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Tour_of_Britain) cycle race began in Liverpool on 7 September, using a city centre circuit to complete 130 km (80.8 mi) of racing.[^cite_note-525] The Tour of Britain took nine stages and finished in London on 14 September.

### Other

See also: [Liverpool Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Marathon)

A 2016 study of UK fitness centres found that, of the top 20 UK urban areas, Liverpool had the highest number of leisure and sports centres per capita, with 4.3 centres per 100,000 of the city population.[^cite_note-526]

## Media

The city has one daily newspaper: the *[Echo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Echo)*, published by [Reach plc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reach_plc). *[The Liverpool Daily Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Liverpool_Daily_Post)* was also published until 2013. The UK's first online-only weekly newspaper called *[Southport Reporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southport_Reporter)* (*Southport and [Mersey Reporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mersey_Reporter)*), is also one of the many other news outlets that cover the city. The independent media organisation *The Post*[^cite_note-527] also covers Liverpool, while [*Nerve* magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nerve_\(British_magazine\)) publishes articles and reviews of cultural events.

[Liverpool TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_TV) is a local television station serving Liverpool City Region and surrounding areas. The station is owned and operated by Made Television Ltd and forms part of a group of eight local TV stations. It broadcasts from studios and offices in Liverpool.



[St Johns Beacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St_Johns_Beacon), former home of [Hits Radio Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hits_Radio_Liverpool) and [Greatest Hits Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greatest_Hits_Radio) from 2000 to 2024

The [ITV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITV_\(TV_network\)) region which covers Liverpool is [ITV Granada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ITV_Granada). In 2006, the Television company opened a new newsroom in the Royal Liver Building. Granada's regional news broadcasts were produced at the [Royal Albert Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Dock,_Liverpool) News Centre during the 1980s and 1990s.[^cite_note-itvnorthwestnews-528] The [BBC](19344654.md) also opened a new newsroom on Hanover Street in 2006.

ITV's daily magazine programme *[This Morning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/This_Morning_\(TV_series\))* was broadcast from studios at [Royal Albert Dock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Albert_Dock,_Liverpool) until 1996, when production was moved to London. Granada's short-lived shopping channel "Shop!" was also produced in Liverpool until it was cancelled in 2002.[^cite_note-529]

Liverpool is the home of the TV production company [Lime Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lime_Pictures), formerly Mersey Television, which produced the now-defunct soap operas *[Brookside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brookside_\(television_programme\))* and *[Grange Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grange_Hill)*. It also produces the soap opera *[Hollyoaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollyoaks)*, which was formerly filmed in [Chester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester) and began on [Channel 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_4) in 1995. All three series were/are largely filmed in the [Childwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childwall) area of Liverpool.

Radio stations include [BBC Radio Merseyside](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Radio_Merseyside), [Hits Radio Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hits_Radio_Liverpool), [Greatest Hits Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greatest_Hits_Radio_Liverpool_&_The_North_West), [Capital Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Liverpool), [In Demand Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_Demand_Radio) and [Liverpool Live Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liverpool_Live_Radio).[^cite_note-530]

Liverpool has also featured in films;[^cite_note-531] see [List of films set in Liverpool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_set_in_Liverpool) for some of them. In films the city has "doubled" for London, Paris, New York, Chicago, Moscow, Dublin, Venice and Berlin.[^cite_note-Ten_facts_about_Liverpool-44][^cite_note-532]

### Consulates

The first overseas [consulate of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consulate_of_the_United_States,_Liverpool) was opened in Liverpool in 1790, and it remained operational for almost two centuries.[^cite_note-536] Today, a large number of consulates are located in the city serving Chile, [Denmark](76972.md), [Estonia](28222445.md), [Finland](10577.md), France, Germany, [Hungary](13275.md), [Iceland](14531.md), Italy, [Netherlands](21148.md), [Norway](21241.md), [Romania](25445.md), Sweden and [Thailand](30128.md). Tunisian & Ivory Coast Consulates are located in the neighbouring [Metropolitan Borough of Sefton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Borough_of_Sefton).
